The reference's response to a mismatched or unknown claim is unrecorded; refusing rather than ignoring is ours. *Evidence: internal/events/inbound.go (threadClaim), route.go (RouteInbound); TestInboundThreadClaimValidation; TestSendValidationSweep ("thread id rejected")* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference refuses or ignores a mismatched or unknown thread claim).*
- **POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events — who may post a `user.tool_result` (confirmed 2026-09-02, #662)** — **Converged for management credentials; one credential wider than the reference.** The reference admits a `user.tool_result` by the credential that signed the request: an environment key posting one on a `self_hosted` session was answered 200, and a Console session posting one was refused **403 `permission_error`** "events[0]: `user.tool_result` may only be sent with environment credentials (the self-hosted runner's Session-Instance JWT); an API key or Console session cannot post this event type" — on that `self_hosted` session and, with the identical body, on a `cloud` one. This platform gated by environment kind instead — a 400 `invalid_request_error` on a `cloud` session and no credential test at all on a `self_hosted` one, where a management `x-api-key` answering a call the worker was to answer was accepted — and registered neither half. It now refuses a management credential, the `x-api-key` and the human identity lane alike, with that 403 on any session, a `user.tool_result` anywhere in a batch refusing the whole batch with nothing appended; the session's environment key is admitted. What an environment credential meets on a `cloud` session is the INFERRED entry "POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events — an environment credential's `user.tool_result` on a `cloud` session". Three differences remain, all ours. (1) **The per-item sessions token (`wtk_`) is admitted**, where the same recording refused a decoded `sessions_token` with the same 403: the reference worker sends its tool results under the item's sessions token whenever the item's secret carries one (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem), and this platform mints one for every item whose session attaches a memory store, so refusing it would fail every answer that worker posts on those sessions. (2) The message is the reference's sentence with its parenthetical naming this platform's credentials — "(the self-hosted worker's environment key or its sessions token)" — since no Session-Instance JWT exists here. (3) The rule is decided where the batch is normalized, after the session is read, so a management caller's result to an absent session is the 404 and to an archived one the archived 400, and within a batch the first event that fails decides the answer — a malformed event ahead of a `user.tool_result` is that event's 400; every recorded refusal was a one-event batch on a live session, so the reference's order in all three is unobserved. *Evidence: internal/events/inbound.go normalizeOne (the `user.tool_result` arm, ErrEnvironmentCredentialRequired); internal/api/envauth.go credentialFrom; internal/api/events.go sendSessionEvents; internal/api/events_test.go TestToolResultAdmissionIsByCredential, TestToolResultRefusalOrder, TestToolResultTypeSpellingsUnderAManagementKey, TestToolResultOnACloudSession and TestSessionsTokenPostsAToolResult; internal/api/identitylane_test.go TestIdentityLaneCannotPostAToolResult.* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/batch2.json, probes `sessW.send.user.tool_result.env-key` (200), `sessW.send.user.tool_result.console-auth`, `sessW.send.user.tool_result.sessions_token` and `sessT.send.user.tool_result.for-platform-call` (each 403, one body).* *Tracked: #78 (whether the reference still refuses the sessions token its own worker answers under, and which it checks first against an absent session); landed for #662.*
- **Reference surface that arrived in one SDK release — advisor, budgets, list-cost usage, `session.usage`, `redacted`, `session.budget_reached` — registered, not built (plan 35 slice 0)** — The SDK bump of plan 35 slice 0 found every managed-agents type change in one release (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.62.0 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsAdvisorParams and BetaManagedAgentsBudgetLimit and BetaManagedAgentsSessionUsageEvent) and none of it in the platform's scope; each is filed and left unbuilt — the advisor and `redacted` are the two whose refusals are settled rather than pending, below — and where a client can reach it the platform rejects rather than silently drops. **Advisor** (#431): the `{type:"advisor", model}` roster entry is reachable since plan 35 slice 1 lifted the `multiagent` rejection (the entry above) and is **400** `entry type must be "agent" or "self"` (`internal/api/roster.go` parseRosterEntry) — the platform's roster admits the two entry kinds it runs. That 400 is the settled answer, not a placeholder (#431): an advisor is a model the *primary thread* may consult mid-turn — which this platform's brain could do — but the reference gates which models may serve as one against a spec a self-hosted deployment cannot consult (the advisor's `model`: "must be permitted as an advisor for this agent's model", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsAdvisorParams.Model), and nothing has asked for a platform-run advisor. So it stays unbuilt, and refusing beats accepting an entry the platform would silently ignore. The three response unions that admit an advisor do render — a thread's `agent` on every session, an agent's and a session's `multiagent` wherever a roster exists — always in their agent arm; it is the advisor *variant* that never appears. **Budgets** (#432): `budget` on session create/update is **400** via the unknown-key rejection — the CLI's `--budget` lands there; `BudgetLimit`/`BetaMonetaryAmount` have no platform type; the `budget_reached` stop reason and the **`session.usage`** event (list, stream and thread-stream unions) are never emitted (a client POSTing `session.usage` gets `unknown event type`); session `usage` carries the token buckets only, none of `active_seconds` / `list_cost` / `server_tool_use`; deployment `budget` is now its own CONFIRMED entry below rather than a deferral (a create or update carrying it is a 400), and the `session.budget_reached` webhook is undelivered like every other webhook (#261). **`redacted`** (#430): the inbound half is conformance, not a divergence — a `user.message` carrying `{type:"redacted"}` is **400** `content block type "redacted" is not allowed here`, and the reference rejects one the same way ("a user event that includes one is rejected with a 400 error"); `blocksUserMessage` admits the text, image and document the reference documents for that event, and nothing else. The emit half is the divergence, and like the advisor's it is settled rather than pending: the reference emits `{type:"redacted"}` as a placeholder for content withheld by Anthropic model policy, this platform runs no such policy and nothing has asked for one, and no Messages API response it can receive carries the block — it exists only in the SDK's managed-agents files. The generated types are a trap pointing the other way: the *inbound param* union registers a redacted arm for a request the reference itself 400s, so the inbound vocabulary must never be widened from them. What the reference renders for a session that has no budget was the one shape-question left, and a 2026-09-12 recording answered it with `null`; the INFERRED entry below now waits on the code rather than on the shape. *Evidence: AdvisorParams and its roster arm, BudgetLimit, the create/update budget, SessionUsage's new fields and SessionUsageEvent, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsAdvisorParams and BetaManagedAgentsMultiagentRosterEntryParamsUnion.OfBetaManagedAgentsAdvisors and BetaManagedAgentsBudgetLimit and BetaManagedAgentsBudgetLimitParam and BetaSessionNewParams.Budget and BetaSessionUpdateParams.Budget and BetaManagedAgentsSessionUsage.ActiveSeconds and BetaManagedAgentsSessionUsage.ListCost and BetaManagedAgentsSessionUsage.ServerToolUse and BetaManagedAgentsSessionUsageEvent; RedactedBlock, the inbound param union's redacted arm, budget_reached and the agent.message content union, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsRedactedBlock and BetaManagedAgentsRedactedBlockParam and BetaManagedAgentsUserMessageEventParamsContentUnion.OfRedacted and BetaManagedAgentsSessionBudgetReached and BetaManagedAgentsAgentMessageEvent.Content; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betawebhook.go BetaWebhookSessionBudgetReachedEventData; platform.claude.com managed-agents/reference § "Event types" → Agent events (fetched 2026-09-07 — "Redacted blocks appear only in content the platform emits; a user event that includes one is rejected with a 400 error"), and its User events tab giving `user.message` as "text, image, or document content"; internal/api/sessions.go rejectUnknownKeys, internal/events/inbound.go block vocabularies; docs/HISTORY.md (the SDK bump's wire-schema verification record of 2026-08-19)* *Tracked: #432 (until budgets are built, `budget` is rejected on create/update, no `budget_reached` stop reason or `session.usage` event is emitted, and session usage carries the token buckets only).*
- **provider/openai adapter — lossy conversion gaps** — Documented, non-silent lossy gaps confined to internal/provider/openai: Anthropic thinking and redacted_thinking blocks are dropped (no Chat Completions equivalent); a tool_result's is_error boolean is dropped (the error text in content is still forwarded); image blocks are unmapped and fail loudly; a tool_result's `search_result` blocks (a web_search answer) flatten to text — title and source URL on a header line, then the hit's text content — because an OpenAI tool message is a string. The rendering itself (`provider.SearchResultText`) lives in the parent `internal/provider` package, not this one, so provider/anthropic's opt-in `flatten_search_results` route flag (entry below) can share it rather than duplicate it. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/provider` — config-driven model access (slice 4)", docs/history/2026-07.md § "OpenAI-compatible adapter (`internal/provider/openai`)" ('Documented lossy gaps (dropped by design, not silently)')*
- **provider/anthropic adapter — opt-in `flatten_search_results` route flag for an endpoint that rejects search_result in tool_result content (#565)** — Near-zero-conversion by default (CLAUDE.md principle 4): the adapter passes search_result blocks through unchanged. A route may set `flatten_search_results: true` to rewrite each into a text block via `provider.SearchResultText`, shared with provider/openai's flattening above; everything else — sibling blocks, string-form content, a tool_result's own fields, a block the rendering cannot parse — stays untouched. Only title/source/content survive: a search_result's `citations` (a `{enabled}` config) has no text-block equivalent — a text block's own `citations` is a list of citation objects, a shape mismatch — so it and the block's boundaries are dropped. What that costs is model-visible now that `web_search` emits `citations.enabled: true` (#548): the flattened turn carries the text but not the permission to cite it, so this flag — and every `protocol: openai` route, which flattens unconditionally — can move the answer text on a session a conformant Anthropic route would answer differently. Accepted as the price of a turn the endpoint would otherwise refuse. Default off, since a conformant endpoint accepts search_result verbatim; MiniMax's `https://api.minimaxi.com/anthropic` (docs/HISTORY.md § "Web-tools slices 2+3 acceptance") does not, answering `400 invalid params, invalid tool_result content (2013)` when replaying a web_search tool_result. Rejected at config load on protocol: openai, which already flattens. *Evidence: internal/provider/provider.go (NewRegistry's protocol:anthropic-only check); internal/provider/anthropic/searchresult.go; internal/provider/searchresult.go (SearchResultText); checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsSearchResultBlockParam and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — message.go TextBlockParam.Citations; docs/HISTORY.md § "Web-tools slices 2+3 acceptance"; issue #565*
- **provider/openai adapter — stop_reason mapping** — stop_reason is forced to tool_use whenever the stream carried any tool call, regardless of finish_reason (some OpenAI-compatible servers end tool turns with stop/length; honoring that would strand the tool). With no tool calls, finish_reason maps length -> max_tokens, everything else -> end_turn. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/provider` — config-driven model access (slice 4)", docs/history/2026-07.md § "OpenAI-compatible adapter (`internal/provider/openai`)" ('stop_reason is tool_use whenever the stream carried any tool call')*
- **user.interrupt — no span.model_request_end for the turn it ends** — An interrupt that lands while a model request is in flight cancels the turn's work item, so the brain's settlement — which carries the lease as its ownership proof — rolls back in full, and with it the span.model_request_end event that settlement would have committed. The reference emits one: the `ant` CLI's `--event-deltas` help says a model request that ends early "(an error or interrupt) produces no final event — its terminal `span.model_request_end` closes the preview". Ours leaves the span.model_request_start unclosed, the same residue a brain crash leaves (entry below). Closing it from the interrupt's own transaction was evaluated and rejected: it cannot be made airtight (a turn that opens its span *after* the interrupt commits is unreachable from it), and the usage it would report is invented. *Evidence: internal/api/events.go (the interrupt case's CancelSession); internal/brain/brain.go (settlement carries the lease proof); internal/brain/interrupt_test.go (TestInterruptedTurnCommitsNothing pins the log an interrupted turn leaves); checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betasessionevent.go betaSessionsEventsStream.* *Tracked: #68 (delivered).*
- **POST/history `processed_at` for inbound events** — Pending input retains `processed_at: null`; the reference recordings can omit that field instead. No recorded event carries it as a present null — the 2026-09-02 echoes of `user.message`, `user.interrupt` and `user.tool_confirmation` (batch2.json, `sessT.send.interrupt` among them) and pending events in listed history all leave it out — but adopting that needs a coordinated console change, since the console's event schema requires the key (#78, #674). Tool results and approvals now receive a processing timestamp when the thread's ordered processor consumes them, and POST echoes that stored value. The order follow-up records a timestamp in an early POST response for beta while a later GET still omits it, then a new processing timestamp once alpha arrives. We keep one truthful, monotonic processing timestamp, without reproducing the reference's POST/history discrepancy or publication delays. Monotonic here means that within one commit the stamps never run backwards through the commit's list order (#793, #539), and two places adjust a stamp to keep it so. `Log.AppendInTx` raises a non-null stamp that is earlier than the one listed ahead of it to that one, on every append — a turn's `span.model_request_end`, stamped when it is built and listed after the `agent.*` rows stamped at insert, takes their stamp. And a send's answers, stamped by the settlement that runs after the append, are clamped into their list slot, no earlier than the rows listed ahead of them and no later than the first listed behind. Only non-null values move: a pending (null) row is never touched, by either. An adjusted stamp takes a value another row of the same commit holds, and nothing reads the difference: no reader compares two stamp values, every one keys on `processed_at` being null or not. `user.define_outcome` retains its existing processing behavior. *Evidence: recordings commit 16d24be5acdb0ee4729b8051b5363ce17991ed06, 2026-09-19-custom-order-followup/setup.json [77,83,84,87]; internal/api/events.go (sendSessionEvents, the answer clamp), internal/events/log.go (AppendInTx) and internal/events/orderedtools.go; internal/api/processing_order_test.go (stampsRunForward) and answerclamp_test.go. Tracked: #78 (field omission and endpoint timestamp differences).*
- **GET /v1/sessions/{id}/events — list filters and order keyed on created_at/seq, not processed_at** — As checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.61.0 — betasessionevent.go BetaSessionEventListParams, the SDK re-worded the list params: each `created_at[gt/gte/lt/lte]` filter is "Compared against the event's `processed_at` value" and `order` is "ordered by the event's `processed_at`" (the param names and wire shape are unchanged). Ours compares the filters against the `created_at` column and orders by `seq` (≡ created_at order — appends serialize per session), deliberately: this platform's stamping lifecycle (the processed_at entry above) leaves every inbound event the platform does not itself synthesize `processed_at: null` until the consuming turn settles (the interrupt-synthesized results are the entry-above's recorded on-receipt exception), so keying the list on processed_at here would make every queued inbound event invisible to filtered lists mid-turn (NULL fails every comparison — including the user.message that woke the turn), invert causality (an agent.message emitted mid-turn would sort before the user.message that caused it), and require a non-unique initially-NULL sort key under the settlement batch's shared timestamp. Under the reference's own on-receipt stamping model the two orderings coincide for nearly every event, so the practical delta for a reference client is minimal. Inference caveat: the processed_at wording is single-source spec-side (the SDK doc comment and the CLI usage string are generated from the same OpenAPI descriptions — one witness); what the deployed service actually compares, and what it does with a null processed_at, is unrecorded and needs a real managed-agents recording. *Evidence: the BetaSessionEventListParams doc comments, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaSessionEventListParams, the file's only change between the copies checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.59.0 — betasessionevent.go BetaSessionEventListParams and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.61.0 — betasessionevent.go BetaSessionEventListParams, comment-only, proven by the empty non-comment diff; unchanged as far as the copy checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasessionevent.go BetaSessionEventListParams, whose whole diff is empty against the copy checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.63.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaSessionEventListParams, the file byte-identical across that range; identical generated wording, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betasessionevent.go betaSessionsEventsList; internal/api/events.go listSessionEvents (created_at[…] → ListQuery.Created*); internal/events/log.go List (created_at comparisons, ORDER BY seq; "seq order and created_at order agree because appends serialize per session"); internal/api/events_test.go (filters pinned against the created_at column; default order chronological)* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle what the deployed service actually compares these filters against, and what it does with a null processed_at).*
- **Event log — brain crash-window residue** — agent.thinking events and span.model_request_start commit mid-stream, so a brain dying mid-turn can leave a duplicate thinking event or a dangling span start on the log. Replay skips both; accepted as cosmetic. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/brain` + `internal/queue` + state machine — the orchestration loop (slice 5)" ('Known crash-window residue (cosmetic, accepted)')*
- **Environment networking 'limited' (sandbox egress)** — **Carved down by plan 12 slice 4: on the Docker backend with the egress gate opted in (`CONTROLPLANE_URL` + `EXECUTOR_GATE_IMAGE` on the executor), `limited` now means the wire's "only allowed_hosts"** — the sandbox's egress rides the injected `HTTP(S)_PROXY` through the per-session gate, which admits CONNECT and plain-HTTP requests on the environment's `allowed_hosts` and whose owner-match firewall drops everything that bypasses the proxy. Plan 12 slice 4 completed on **both backends** — the K8s provider gained the gate as a native sidecar (slice 4d, PR #198), so a gate-opted-in limited or vault-attached K8s pod now means the same wire "only allowed_hosts" as Docker. The original stricter-than-wire fail-closed posture remains only where the gate does not run: an un-opted-in Docker executor still gives a limited sandbox NetworkMode none (no route out), and a K8s pod *without* the gate opt-in keeps the route-flushing init container. Both `limited` widening flags are now honored, the package-registry one only as far as a recording sized it — see the INFERRED gate-policy entry. *Evidence: internal/sandbox/sandboxtest/egress.go (GatedLimitedEgressOnlyThroughGate: allowed host reachable through the gate, non-allowed refused, direct egress dropped — on a real daemon, both backends); internal/sandbox/docker/docker.go networkMode + internal/sandbox/k8s/k8s.go (ungated limited still none / route-flush); docs/history/2026-07.md § "Vaults plan — archived" and § "Vaults acceptance"* *Tracked: #50 (delivered; BYOC gate delivery = #165).*
- **Built-in file/search tools — path confinement** — No workdir confinement: relative paths resolve against the workdir; absolute paths and absolute glob patterns are accepted. The reference runs tools on the host and canonicalizes/rejects escapes; here the container is the boundary, so a lexical check would be theatre. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/toolset` — the built-in tools (slice 6, third part)" (slice-6 toolset decisions, divergences from the SDK's tools/agenttoolset)*
- **grep built-in tool — implementation strategy** — One implementation over the image's GNU grep (PCRE where available, ERE fallback), not the reference's ripgrep-or-Go-walker pair with two behaviors. Requires GNU coreutils/grep/findutils in the sandbox image; a busybox-only image gets a clear tool error rather than degraded behavior. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/toolset` — the built-in tools (slice 6, third part)"*
- **web_fetch / web_search built-in tools — executor-process execution, pluggable backends** — Implemented (plan 15, #47): both tools execute **in the platform executor's own process on both deployment modes** — never in the sandbox, never on a BYOC worker (the official client toolset implements only the six sandbox tools, so a self_hosted web call reaching a real `ant` worker would fail unknown-tool), and deliberately **not** through the plan-12 egress gate: the reference documents that the environment's networking policy "does not affect the allowed domains for the web_search or web_fetch tools", so a `limited` environment does not constrain them and web egress originates at the executor. Routing is a third internal work kind, `web_exec` — claimed by the executor for **both** environment kinds; `Poll` never serves it (the kind taxonomy is internal, not wire). The backends are config-driven per design principle 4 where the reference's are a managed black box: Tavily-protocol search and Jina-Reader-protocol fetch behind `internal/webtool`'s Searcher/Fetcher seam (`TAVILY_API_KEY`/`JINA_API_KEY`/`WEBSEARCH_BASE_URL`/`WEBFETCH_BASE_URL`); an enabled tool whose backend is unconfigured answers an `is_error` result naming what is missing rather than being hidden from the definitions (`TAVILY_API_KEY` for search; `JINA_API_KEY` **or** `WEBFETCH_BASE_URL` for fetch — the Reader protocol works keyless, but a bare install must not silently egress model-chosen URLs to a public default). Results: `web_search` → one `search_result` block per hit, matching the SDK's `BetaManagedAgentsSearchResultBlock` field-for-field (round-trip-tested; `citations.enabled` is emitted **`true`**, which is what a 2026-09-02 recording caught the reference sending on every one of ten result blocks; it is emitted unconditionally, the agent's own citations config not being surfaced to vary it, and it is model-visible rather than cosmetic, because the flag is what tells the model it may cite the block), zero hits → a single "No results found." text block (the shape the Messages-API web_search docs prescribe for an empty result); `web_fetch` → a text block, where the same recording showed the reference answering with a **`document`** block carrying the page title and a `source` of `type: text` / `media_type: text/plain` — a mismatch, owned by **#547**, and a reviewer must not clear it on this entry's authority (the input schemas and mixed-turn ordering are still the INFERRED entries below; the block choice no longer is). *Evidence: internal/executor/webwork.go; internal/webtool; internal/toolset/definitions.go; internal/queue/queue.go (WebExec); internal/toolset/searchresult_test.go (SDK round-trip, whose fixture keeps the zero `citations.enabled` on purpose — that is what leaves its respjson presence assertion load-bearing, and it pins the block's shape, not the driver's value); internal/executor/webwork_test.go (TestWebSearchAnswersWithSearchResultBlocks pins the emitted `true`, on every block); the six-tool client toolset, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — agenttoolset.go BetaAgentToolset20260401; platform.claude.com managed-agents environments doc (the networking sentence)* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/batch2.json, entry idx 362 (`sessT.events.after-outcome`) — ten `search_result` blocks, every one `citations.enabled` true, and the only entry in the archive carrying the block at all.* *Tracked: #547 (the recorded `web_fetch` document-block mismatch, settled by the 2026-09-02 recording rather than left to a future one); landed for #47, #548.*
- **Web tools — the reference's per-tool domain lists refused; the fence is operator-side here (plan 36 slice 0)** — As checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsWebFetchToolConfig and BetaManagedAgentsWebSearchToolConfig, the SDK put the web tools' configuration on the wire: `web_fetch`'s config gained `allowed_domains`, `blocked_domains` and `max_content_tokens`, `web_search`'s gained `allowed_domains`, `blocked_domains` and `user_location`, each list documented as plain hostnames — `web_fetch`'s "no scheme, port, or path", `web_search`'s "no scheme or port; an optional path suffix is accepted" — at most 64 entries, an empty list rejected, and the two lists mutually exclusive. This platform's strict key check answers all four with a 400 and keeps the fence operator-side: `WEBTOOL_ALLOWED_DOMAINS` on the executor, entries in the wire's `allowed_hosts` grammar matched by the shared `egress.HostSet` and validated at startup, a blocked `web_fetch` host answered as a tool error before any fetch, a search hit outside the list dropped. Refusing beats accepting-and-ignoring here for the reason the field exists: a silently dropped `allowed_domains` reads to its author as a fence in force. The operator list is also the outer bound any per-agent list would sit inside, so honoring the wire fields is additive work — #481. **This entry replaces the inference the bump falsified.** It read "per-tool allowed domains exist in the reference, configured by no wire field", inferring a console-side mechanism because no field in the then-pinned SDK carried them; it is rewritten rather than deleted so the reversal stays auditable. The environments doc's sentence it rested on — networking "does not affect the allowed domains for the web_search or web_fetch tools" — now has a field behind it instead of a console. What the reference's own list holds is still unobserved; how it answers a fenced call is not, and the comparison against our text and our drop is drawn below. *Evidence: the response shapes, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsWebFetchToolConfig and BetaManagedAgentsWebSearchToolConfig; the params' documented grammar, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsWebFetchToolConfigParams.AllowedDomains and BetaManagedAgentsWebFetchToolConfigParams.BlockedDomains and BetaManagedAgentsWebSearchToolConfigParams.AllowedDomains and BetaManagedAgentsWebSearchToolConfigParams.BlockedDomains; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsUserLocation; the single struct that carried no domains field, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.63.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfig, and that the union split deleted, absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfig; platform.claude.com managed-agents environments doc (Networking); internal/toolset/definitions.go rejectConfigKeys; internal/egress/hostset.go ValidateHostEntry (the one grammar the vault API and this list share), internal/executor/webwork.go:301 (the blocked-host tool error); internal/executor/webwork_test.go TestWebFetchOutsideAllowedDomainsAnswersIsError, TestWebSearchFiltersHitsOutsideAllowedDomains* **Recorded — a blocked domain answers the model as an ordinary `is_error` tool result, and the reference's own text names the field that blocked it.** The fetch is permitted first: the `agent.tool_use` for `web_fetch` on the blocked host carries `evaluated_permission: "allow"`, so a per-tool domain list is not a permission decision but a failure inside the call. It lands as one `agent.tool_result` with `is_error: true` and a single text block — `Web fetch error: url_not_allowed — example.com is a domain this agent is blocked from fetching (blocked_domains). Retrying this or other URLs on that domain will not work.` — and no accompanying error event: the turn continued, the model quoted the text back, and the session settled idle in the same turn. The refused call left `usage.server_tool_use.web_fetch_requests` at `0`, and a later pass measured a *successful* unfenced fetch at `0` too, so that counter distinguishes nothing about fetch; its search twin does move, reading `1` on a fenced search as on an unfenced one. **The event shape this platform already produces is the same one; its cause deliberately is not** — the executor answers a fenced host with an `is_error` text result before the fetcher is called, but the fence is the operator's `WEBTOOL_ALLOWED_DOMAINS` rather than a per-agent list, so the message names that variable and carries neither the reference's `url_not_allowed` code nor its futility hint. The recorded text is what #481 should emit once the wire fields are honored, not a correction to make while they are refused. The same probe family also confirmed on the wire the three list constraints the SDK only documents — the two lists mutually exclusive, an empty list refused as ambiguous, a 65th entry refused — each an `invalid_request_error` 400. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `agent.create.web_fetch-blocked`, `session.create.blocked-domain`, `session.events.send.blocked-domain` and `session.events.list.blocked-domain`, with the three refusals `agent.create.web_fetch-both-lists`, `.web_fetch-empty-list` and `.web_fetch-65-entries`. A `web_search` agent carrying `allowed_domains` was created and never run in that pass.* **Recorded — the two tools do not answer a fence the same way.** A fenced `web_search` filters silently: `is_error: false`, no `url_not_allowed`, the turn ends `end_turn`, and the fence shows only in the hit list — `allowed_domains` narrowed ten search blocks to three, all on the listed domain, and `blocked_domains` kept ten while dropping every hit on the listed one. So the two behaviors #481 must produce are an error result for a fenced fetch and a shortened list for a fenced search, not one shape for both. A fenced search is also a counted request, `web_search_requests` reading `1` there as on the unfenced baseline. The same pass recorded the accepted side of the list bound — exactly **64** entries on both lists and both tools, each echo retaining all 64 — and a positive `max_content_tokens` in force: `100` returned a 339-character exact prefix of the uncapped 49,096, with no truncation flag, marker or error. Which stage of the search implementation drops the hits is not located by this, and the fenced-search URLs are search-provider output rather than pages verified reachable. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-12-followups/web-runtime.json, events idx 19 (unfenced baseline), 20 (`allowed_domains`), 21 (`blocked_domains`), [15..18] (the 64-entry acceptances), [22, 23] (the capped and uncapped fetch), with web-summary.json for URL lists, usage and content hashes.* *Tracked: #481 (accepting and honoring the four wire fields — the recorded blocked-fetch text and the recorded silent search filtering are the two answers to match); enforcement landed for #225.*
- **Oversized tool output — sandbox tools spill to a file (path and preview ours); web answers still truncate** — The reference documents the behaviour and not its shapes: "When a tool output exceeds 100,000 characters … it is automatically written to a file in the sandbox. The model receives a truncated preview with the file path." The sandbox tools do that (#226, plan 18) — past `toolset.MaxOutputBytes` the output lands NUL-sanitized at `/tmp/tool_outputs/<tool_use_id>.txt` and the preview keeps the head with a marker naming the file. **The path convention and the preview text are ours and INFERRED**, the reference publishing neither. "Whole" is honest rather than absolute: it means everything `Sandbox.Exec` retained, its 1 MiB per-stream guard having already drained the excess. A failed spill falls back to the plain trailer, costing the call nothing. **`read` alone never spills** — its content already sits at the path the model just named, and a spilled copy would chain with no fixed point, since every spill file exceeds the cap by construction. **An MCP answer spills too** (plan 29 slice 4c) into the same directory under the same one-file-per-call-id convention, so a model that has learned where its output goes is right whichever tool produced it — but under its own sentence, because the sandbox tools promise the "full output" and an answer that also carried an image carried it somewhere the file does not go. What spills is the answer as the model would have read it, free of the caps its inline blocks are held to; an image or blob is named rather than written, and an answer with no text spills nothing. **The trigger is whether the answer arrived whole, reported by the renderer rather than inferred from a length** — a length answers it in neither direction, since one already-capped block is exempt from the budget (so two 60 KB text blocks both arrive) while five thousand short blocks sit under the budget in text and over it marshalled. Labels a block is *described* by are capped at 2 KiB, the stance a document title always took, because uncapped one would eat the budget and report no truncation of its own. **A spill creates nothing**: it writes through `sandbox.Provider.Attach`, Provision's read-only half, which answers `ErrNotFound` for a session with no running sandbox. That seam is the point — `Provision` is the platform's *healing* path, right for a caller about to run tools and wrong for one writing a file it could skip, and provisioning here would put a container behind every cloud session declaring an MCP server and point its model at a file it has no tool to open. Two bounds are honest: `/tmp` is not a checkpoint root, so a reaped session returns without the file while the notice naming it stays on the append-only log; and a **`self_hosted` session spills nothing**, its sandbox being reachable only through a work API with no MCP surface. The **web tools deliberately keep truncating**, and the two do it differently: `web_fetch` truncates with the plain trailer, while `web_search`'s structured answer is **pruned** against the same budget with **no marker** — a snippet is admitted whole or omitted, and a hit whose title and URL alone exceed the remaining budget is dropped — which is a different observable answer shape, not truncation. Neither spills: their driver runs with no sandbox provisioned by plan 15's design, provisioning one to store an answer would couple the web pass back into sandbox lifecycle, and web content, unlike a command's output, is re-fetchable. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/tools (the spill sentence, fetched 2026-08-01); internal/toolset/toolset.go and bash.go, internal/executor/mcpspill.go, internal/sandbox/sandbox.go (Provider.Attach, and the amended "there is no Attach" note explaining why a caller that must not heal needed one); the sandbox contract suite pins Attach's three cases, and around sixteen named tests in internal/executor/mcpspill_test.go pin the rest — TestMCPAnswerThatLosesBlocksSpillsEvenWhenItsTextIsSmall, TestMCPOversizedAnswerThatArrivedWholeSpillsNothing, TestMCPSpillOnASessionWithNoSandboxProvisionsNone, TestMCPSelfHostedOversizedAnswerTruncatesWithoutSpilling and the others state the rules directly.* *Tracked: #226 (delivered; the sandbox spill), #45 (delivered; the MCP spill).*
- **bash tool result — stdout/stderr interleaving** — stdout and stderr are captured separately and the result carries stderr after stdout whole, not interleaved. The reference's bash runs on a PTY and merges them; matching that would require giving the sandbox a PTY. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/toolset` — the built-in tools (slice 6, third part)"*
- **bash tool — cross-call shell state persistence** — Statefulness (cd, exports, functions, options; restart:true wipes) is provided via a per-call snapshot/restore on the container's writable layer, not a resident shell. Documented divergences from a resident shell, each pinned by a test: the jobs table does not carry, plain (non-exported) variables do not carry, traps do not carry, xtrace does not carry, a timed-out call's mutations are dropped, a call whose snapshot never completes keeps the previous call's state, and the timeout bounds the whole call (restore+command+snapshot). *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/sandbox/shell` — the persistent bash tool (slice 6, second part)" (internal/sandbox/shell; 'Divergences from a resident shell — all of them, each pinned by a test')*
- **write / edit built-in tools — atomicity** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#71).** Both backends write the way the reference does: the bytes land under a temporary name in the target's own directory and are renamed into place, so a mid-transfer failure leaves the target as it was. This entry replaces an earlier one recording the opposite ("Not atomic … atomicity deferred to the sandbox backend"), corrected rather than deleted so the convergence stays auditable. Writing by rename then had to be made to answer the same questions the reference's own write does, and four residuals it opened are closed: the target's permission bits are preserved and a created file lands 0644 on both backends (#204, #212) — not left to the image's umask or to a parent's default POSIX ACL (#213), both of which decide a created file's mode where nothing sets it; a target that cannot be renamed onto at all, a bind-mounted file or a device node, is refused with `ErrNotReplaceable` before the move rather than silently supplanted (#205); and a write the sandbox cannot land is the model's error on both backends (#306, plan 23), as every write failure is in the reference's own `tools/agenttoolset`, which returns each through `errorf` with no error-code taxonomy on the wire — so compatibility constrains the wording rather than a code, and the reasons are normalized the way its own `fsErrorMessage` table does. **One residual stays open (#209)**: docker's temporary file is extracted by the daemon rather than created by the sandbox user, so an image whose default user is not root cannot chmod it and the write lands 0644 where k8s preserves the target's mode. One wording residual is deliberate — the reference prefixes a parent-creation failure with a `mkdir:` segment where both routes here carry the reason alone, since the sandbox's exit-20 protocol does not say which create was refused and the raw-passthrough tail cannot match byte-for-byte anyway. *Evidence: internal/sandbox/sandbox.go (WriteFile's doc states the four consequences of writing by rename), internal/sandbox/filefault.go, bulkwrite.go, k8s/k8s.go and docker/docker.go carry the mechanics; the shared contract suite pins the behaviour on both backends (WriteThatFailsLeavesTheTargetAlone, WriteOntoDeviceNode, WriteKeepsTheTargetsMode and the rest), with the umask and ACL cases in k8s_internal_test.go; the measurements behind each closure — GNU tar and BusyBox, root and non-root, permissive and restrictive ACLs — are in docs/history/2026-07.md § "Sandbox file primitives (#71) — review-hardening record".* *Tracked: #209 (non-root docker images).*
- **write / edit built-in tools — permission bits of a replaced file** — We preserve the target's mode; the SDK's own host-side runner writes a fixed 0644. The SDK's `tools/agenttoolset` — which internal/toolset names as the reference implementation of these six tools — implements both tools as `atomicWriteFile(path, data, 0o644)`, chmod'ing its temporary file to that constant before renaming, so a file it rewrites comes back 0644 whatever it was. We follow the Claude Code harness instead, which stats the target and carries its mode over, because the workflow that decides it is ordinary: `chmod +x` a script in bash, `edit` it, run it (#204). Recorded because the ladder makes an unrecorded mismatch against a reference checkout a finding, not because anything wire is at stake — the typed schema for `write`/`edit` carries no mode field at all, so this is host-runner behavior on both sides. *Evidence: atomicWriteFile, the 0o644 constant and the Chmod-then-Rename (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — fs.go execWrite and execEdit and atomicWriteFile); claude-code-source src/utils/file.ts:390-437 (stat target → chmodSync(tempPath, targetMode) → renameSync); internal/toolset/toolset.go (the package's own divergence list), internal/sandbox/filefault.go (PreserveModeShell)*
- **Environment worker keys — issuance** — Key issuance is a platform-owned primitive, and its model is the reference console's rather than rotate-on-mint: `IssueEnvironmentKey` generates the secret itself (256 CSPRNG bits behind an `sk-map-env01-` prefix), stores only its SHA-256 hash, carries a name so one host's credential can be told from another's, and expires a year out — so an environment holds as many live keys as it has hosts, and `RevokeEnvironmentKey` retires one alone (idempotent, and scoped to the owning environment so a key id is neither revocable nor confirmable from another). Migration 0021 retired `environment_keys_one_live` (0013) to make several live keys representable; `key_hash` stays UNIQUE, so a key value still binds to one environment for life. **This entry replaces one recording the previous model** — "EnsureEnvironmentKey: hash-only, revoke-others-on-re-mint … Currently there is also no operator surface invoking it" — corrected rather than deleted so the change stays auditable. The reference mints environment keys off-wire on its console's private backend — no SDK/CLI wire endpoint creates one — so a self-hostable platform must own this; the consuming side (Bearer -> environment) stays wire-locked by the real ant beta:worker. The prefix deliberately is not the reference's `sk-ant-oat01-`: this platform is not that OAuth infrastructure, and a key that reads like an Anthropic credential invites being pasted into ANTHROPIC_API_KEY. Nothing anywhere parses the format. The operator surface calling the primitive is the console-API dialect in the next entry. *Evidence: internal/api/envkeys.go, internal/store/migrations/0021_environment_keys_named.sql, internal/api/envkeys_test.go (per-host coexistence, hash-only storage, scoped idempotent revoke); docs/plan/30_environment-keys-console-issuance.md § "Ground truth" (the 2026-08-10 live observation of the reference console's issuance flow — named keys, a one-year expiry, per-key revoke); anthropic-cli: worker only consumes --environment-key* *Landed for #43.*
- **`/api/oauth/organizations/{organization_id}/environments/{environment_id}/tokens[…]` — the console-API dialect** — The operator surface that issues, lists and revokes environment keys is **off the `/v1` wire**, in an `/api/` namespace mirrored segment-for-segment from the reference console's own private backend (observed live 2026-08-10): `POST …/tokens` issues, `GET …/tokens` lists, `POST …/tokens/{token_id}/revoke` retires one. Mirroring rather than inventing it is the point (the surface is ours for the entry above's reason). **`/v1` gains nothing**: a surface diff must show zero new wire routes. Four things inside the dialect deliberately differ from what was observed. **The secret's prefix** is `sk-map-env01-`, not the reference's `sk-ant-oat01-` (rationale in the entry above). **The `id`** is `envkey_…`, not the reference's bare UUID, and it deliberately stays out of `domain.knownPrefixes`, so `{token_id}` takes a local `domain.ValidWithPrefix` check instead. **`authorization_details` is not emitted**: the reference returns RFC 9396 rich-authorization metadata from its own OAuth infrastructure, whose `actions` list is demonstrably narrower than what the key actually authorizes per the SDK, and copying a claim this platform neither generates nor enforces would be a lie in a security-adjacent field; revisit if per-key scoping ever becomes real. **The error envelope** is the house `{"type":"error",…}` shape; the dialect's own error bodies are unobserved (see the INFERRED entry). Auth is the management `x-api-key`, and an environment key presented here is a 401, pinned by test. What routes it to that lane is **not** a blanket `/v1/` prefix rule — the gate lane's fixed-path equality falsifies that, and a future off-`/v1` lane must re-derive its own answer rather than assume — but an explicit `/api/` arm in `dispatchAuth` resolving exactly as the default management lane. "Console-only" therefore means off the wire and built for the console, not cryptographically confined: one management credential cannot distinguish its callers. *Evidence: internal/api/consoleapi.go — its header comment carries the auth derivation this row drops and the BFF deployment note, revokeEnvironmentKey's the `envkey_`-versus-`checkID` reasoning; internal/api/server.go (the three routes, their 405 fallbacks, the `isConsolePath` arm); internal/api/consoleapi_test.go TestConsoleKeyRoutesRequireManagementAuth and TestConsoleIssuedKeyDrivesAWorkerAndIsRevocable; docs/plan/30_environment-keys-console-issuance.md §§ "Ground truth" (the 2026-08-10 capture — paths, bodies, `expires_in: 31536000`, the `authorization_details` block — plus the SDK evidence for the wider authority) and "The console-API surface"* *Landed for #43.*
- **Environment key expiry — an expired key is indistinguishable from an unknown one** — `authenticateEnvironmentKey` answers revoked, expired and never-existed identically: one 401 carrying one message, so the auth lane cannot be used as an oracle for which of the three a probing client hit. A key minted before keys carried expiries has a NULL `expires_at` and never expires — 0021 deliberately does not backfill one, since dating an existing key's expiry from its `created_at` would retro-expire credentials that are authenticating right now. Unobservable against the reference: its keys are OAuth access tokens whose expiry its own token infrastructure enforces, and no wire shape distinguishes the failures. *Evidence: internal/api/envauth.go (authenticateEnvironmentKey's expires_at predicate), internal/api/envkeys_test.go TestEnvironmentKeyExpiryStopsAuthentication (asserts the two messages are equal) and TestEnvironmentKeyWithoutExpiryStillAuthenticates* *Landed for #43.*
- **Work items (poll / get / list / stats) — when one is enqueued, and what the queue therefore holds** — The payload is the reference's shape field for field — `data` is `{id: "sesn_…", type: "session"}`, `BetaSessionWorkData`'s two fields and nothing else — and an item's *duration* is its holder's business on both sides, not the control plane's. What diverges is **when one is enqueued, and therefore how many a session produces**. There an item is the session: the docs say Anthropic "enqueues the session as a work item" and the worker "claims work items from that queue, spawns an execution context for each one, downloads the agent's skills …, runs the tool calls, and posts the results back". The SDK adds the trigger — items "are queued when sessions are created or when long-dormant sessions receive new messages" — and its own worker consumes one that way: the poller acks on yield while `handleItem` heartbeats the lease and a `SessionToolRunner` streams that session's events and answers the calls its own toolset owns (an unowned name gets no result, and `agent.mcp_tool_use` is left to the server), until the session terminates or stays idle past `MaxIdle` — 60s after `end_turn` — whereupon the item is force-stopped and the loop polls for the next. The `ant` CLI's `beta:worker poll` *is* that worker, and says so in its usage line ("run an in-process session tool runner for each session"). Here nothing is enqueued for a session as such. Of the five kinds sharing `work_items` the work API serves exactly one — a `tool_exec` item on a `self_hosted` environment; `model_turn`, `web_exec`, `outputs_harvest` and `mcp_exec` are claimed in-process and never reach the wire — and one is created only by a commit that leaves a **runnable sandbox** tool call standing on that session: the settlement that appends such a call, the confirmation that releases an `always_ask` gate (which until then suspends the whole turn and enqueues nothing), the executor's own settlement chaining past a platform-executed call — web or MCP alike — when sandbox calls remain, and the re-arm that follows a stopped `tool_exec` whose session still has runnable ones, a worker's own stop and the next poll's finalization of an abandoned wind-down alike. None of them is session creation or a new message: what those enqueue, when they enqueue anything, is a `model_turn`, which no worker polls. A web, MCP or delegation call puts nothing on the wire at all. And the count is not one per settlement, because the item is session-keyed and deduped while live: a session holds at most one at a time, and a settlement that lands while one is live is absorbed by it rather than adding another. One consequence a client can see without a recording: a session that never runs a sandbox tool never appears in `…/work`'s listing, and one that does appears there first at its first *confirmed* sandbox call rather than at create. (`…/work/stats` will not show it — its counters are environment-wide, and `depth` counts unreserved queued rows only.) The argument is this platform's division of labour: the session's runner is the brain, not the worker. A `model_turn` calls the model with the deployment's credentials and appends to the event log, and a BYOC worker by design holds neither a database handle nor a model credential — so the session is not a thing that can be handed to it, and the queue names the only unit that can be: sandbox work that is ready to run. The `Skills materialization` entry above is where the shape's cost lands: the reference re-extracts every skill per claimed item, so a queue that hands out more items per session charges it more often — once per activity span there, once per item a worker claims and ends here. **A reference-written worker does not break on this, and that is observed rather than argued**: the real `ant beta:worker poll` has claimed this platform's items and settled them in every acceptance that has driven it — the slice-8 one reconciling the session by listing its events, running `bash` and posting the `user.tool_result`; the plan-30 console-key one logging `work_type=session` and exiting on its own `max_idle=1m0s`; the skills one running the SDK's `SetupSkills` against our registry with "no CLI or SDK accommodation anywhere"; the web-tools one tolerating, in a mixed web+bash turn, a call it did not own rather than erroring; and the plan-35 coordinator one, where that thread-unaware runner served a *child thread's* call without knowing threads exist. What absorbs the difference is what the first of those recorded — the runner reconciles by listing the session's events, so an item is a wake-up naming a session and the calls come off the log, never off the item — and nothing here bounds a holder to one pass: our own worker force-stops after its drain by choice, which is the client-side difference the `self_hosted` session view / coordinator-mode BYOC worker entry below already records, not a second wire mismatch. One residual, claimed neither way: whether `data` ever carries more than the session arm. The wired surface carries one: `BetaSelfHostedWork.Data` is typed `BetaSessionWorkData` alone, the public API reference for poll, retrieve and list documents that arm and no other, and the spec's `data` is a plain `$ref` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaSelfHostedWork.properties.data) in a spec with no health-check schema at all (absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaHealthCheckWorkData). Against that, the SDK generates an unreferenced `BetaHealthCheckWorkData` ("assessing the health of containers where work occurs … probing connectivity to required services") whose discriminator is `healthcheck`, and its hand-written worker says in the present tense that the queue sends one — "The queue also sends "healthcheck" items, which the generated type does not model" (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem) — an item a worker test scripts (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker_test.go TestEnvironmentWorker_SkipsNonSessionItem). All that worker does with one is force-stop it: it fetches and serves no session and probes nothing. It is the `ant` CLI's default `beta:worker poll` path, and the CLI's `--on-work` path skips one too ("unsupported work type, skipping"). That is the reference's authors saying the queue sends a second arm, not code anticipating one — but it is not a recording, and the recordings cut the other way only weakly: every `claimed work` line in the managed-agents-wire-recordings archive at 16d24be reads `work_type` `session`, and `healthcheck` appears nowhere in it but a snapshot of this platform's own issues, which bounds how often a health check comes, not whether. So this stays a question for the recording. This platform emits and holds the session arm only — a work row belongs to a session by schema — and a reference worker loses nothing by that, since all it does with a health check is stop it. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/self-hosted-sandboxes ("Anthropic enqueues the session as a work item"; the worker "spawns an execution context for each one … runs the tool calls") and api/beta/environments/work poll and retrieve (the same "queued when sessions are created" sentence, which the list page does not carry; `data` documented as the session arm alone on all three), fetched 2026-08-23; the BetaSelfHostedWork doc comment, Data typed BetaSessionWorkData, the unreferenced BetaHealthCheckWorkData (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork and BetaSelfHostedWork.Data and BetaHealthCheckWorkData), DefaultMaxIdle 60s, an unowned tool name gets no result, mcp_tool_use left to the server (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessiontoolrunner.go DefaultMaxIdle and SessionToolRunner.execute and SessionToolRunner.streamLoop), ack on yield (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go WorkPoller.Next) and Run's one-item-at-a-time loop, handleItem's runner exiting on ErrSessionTerminated/ErrIdleTimeout, then force-stopping (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.Run and EnvironmentWorker.handleItem), anthropic-cli's usage line and --on-work data.type guard (checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — worker.go workerPollCommand and newOnWorkRunner); internal/queue/queue.go (the five Kind constants and the Claim/Poll split, Enqueue's live-item dedup), internal/store/migrations/0001_init.sql (work_items.session_id NOT NULL), internal/queue/lifecycle.go (workAPIScope), internal/queue/stats.go (depth counts unreserved queued rows, environment-wide), internal/api/workapi.go (workData, toWire, pollWork, and stopWork's re-arm), internal/api/sessions.go (the model_turn enqueued only for a non-empty initial_events) and internal/api/events.go (startWorkCycle, which a message under way does not reach), internal/brain/brain.go (escalate to ToolExec on a runnable agent.tool_use; the askIDs gate that enqueues nothing), internal/executor/drain.go (settleDrain, which chains a tool_exec past a web or MCP pass), internal/worker/lease.go (the worker's own force-stop after its drain); docs/HISTORY.md and docs/history/2026-07.md (the real-`ant beta:worker` acceptance records); managed-agents-wire-recordings at 16d24be (every `claimed work` line; no `healthcheck` outside 2026-09-12/platform-open-issues.json); CLAUDE.md "Core architecture"* One thing the queue's contents are recorded to protect, and since #546 do here too: the reference refuses `DELETE /v1/environments/{id}` on a `self_hosted` environment that still holds queued work — "Either archive the environment first to allow the queue to drain, or use force=true to delete immediately" — and this platform now answers the same 409 in the same sentence; what `force=true` lifts here, and what it cannot, is the `DELETE /v1/environments/{id} — the self-hosted queue refusal and force=true` entry below. *Tracked: #78 (what the reference's own poll hands out — whether `data` ever carries a non-session arm, and whether one session ever produces more than one item there); landed for #457.*
- **GET /v1/environments/{id}/work/poll — block_ms** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#74).** The poll now long-polls as the reference's does: an empty poll subscribes to a `map_work_items` NOTIFY (fired by `queue.Enqueue` on the enqueuer's own handle — commit-delivered inside a transaction — keyed by environment id) and is held open until work arrives, the window lapses, or the client disconnects, with a final re-poll at the deadline because a wake can race the timer. The window semantics follow the SDK's recorded contract: absent means non-blocking, the server caps the window at 999ms (our over-cap values clamp to that ceiling), and an explicit 0 is rejected — non-blocking is expressed by omission ("the server caps this at 999" and "the server rejects an explicit 0", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go pollBlockMillis and WorkPollerOptions.BlockMs). Two edges are our reading rather than the record's: the clamp itself, for a client that actually sends more than 999 (the reference client's default sends exactly 999, and a caller-configured BlockMs is forwarded unvalidated but unrecorded against the real server — so whether it clamps or rejects an over-cap value is unknown; the strictest reading of the param doc's "Must be 1-999" would reject, and a value too large for a machine integer does take our 400 path rather than the clamp), and extending the 0-rejection to negative, present-but-empty (`?block_ms=`, which is not omission), unparseable, and repeated values — the reference's exact answer for all of these is unrecorded. Availability that arrives without an enqueue (a lapsed reservation or lease reclaim) has no NOTIFY and is discovered by the next poll, at most one ≤999ms window late; the worker's `EmptyPollSleep` deliberately stays on top, because the reference client sleeps between empty polls the same way. **This entry replaces the one recording the non-blocking poll** — "block_ms is accepted but the poll is non-blocking (returns immediately) … True long-poll on a work_items NOTIFY is deferred" — corrected rather than deleted so the convergence stays auditable. *Evidence: internal/api/workapi.go (pollWork, blockWindow), internal/queue/queue.go (Enqueue's NotifyWorkEnqueued), internal/events/log.go + broker.go (channelWork); internal/api/workapi_test.go TestWorkPollBlockMs\*, internal/queue/queue_test.go TestEnqueueNotifiesWorkChannelOnCommit; the BlockMs contract (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go WorkPollerOptions.BlockMs)* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference clamps or rejects an over-cap block_ms, and what it answers to a negative, empty, unparseable or repeated value); landed for #74.*
- **POST …/work/{work_id}/stop — response shape** — Success is a bodiless 204 with no Content-Type. This diverges from the **published spec** — the public Stop Work reference and the SDK's api.md/generated method all declare a BetaSelfHostedWork return — deliberately, to match the **deployed service**, which the SDK's own work poller describes in present tense (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go stopWork): "Today the server returns 204 with no body / no Content-Type, and the strict Go decoder errors … for what is actually a successful call", a Go-only strictness (TS/Python decode 204 fine) worked around with WithResponseBodyInto under a TODO asking for the spec to stop declaring a body "that the server never sends". The three spec-side witnesses are not independent — docs, api.md and the signature are all downstream of the one spec that erratum names as wrong. **This entry replaces an earlier CONFIRMED one asserting the opposite** (200 + the updated work object, on the grounds that a 204 makes the SDK's typed decoder error): that measurement was real but it measured the *client* and concluded something about the *service*. Retained rather than deleted so the reversal stays auditable. Our BYOC worker adopts the reference's own bypass. A conflicting stop (already past the requested transition) is still 409 invalid_request_error, which the reference worker ignores. Only a recording against a real managed-agents endpoint can close the spec-vs-runtime gap. *Evidence: the comment and the WithResponseBodyInto workaround (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — poller.go stopWork), HandleStop writes StatusNoContent (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker_test.go TestWorkPoller_ThreadsCustomRequestOptions); contrary spec-side evidence: platform.claude.com Stop Work reference and api.md's Stop row, a BetaSelfHostedWork return (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — api.md BetaEnvironmentWorkService.Stop); internal/worker/lease_test.go TestWorkerForceStopAcceptsNoContent reproduces the decoder error verbatim when the bypass is removed; the real `ant beta:environments:work stop` drives the 204 server clean; CHANGELOG.md § [0.2.0] → Fixed* *Tracked: #78 (recording to confirm); reversal landed for #27.*
- **Work-item identity — a fresh `work_` id per re-hand-out** — The wire carries no ownership proof — no generation/version field on the work object, stop's body `{force}` only — so re-offering the same id let a hung-then-revived worker's force-stop land on the *replacement's* reclaimed item and re-strand the session. This platform rotates the identity instead: `queue.Poll` mints a new `work_` id on every re-offer — the lapsed un-acked reservation and the dead-worker `starting`/`active` reclaim — so a stale worker's ack, heartbeat and stop 404 on an id that no longer exists, not the live item. Those three 404s are safe by different routes, not one: the heartbeat reads a lost lease and cancels the run; a stop is logged against the session (its work id unresolvable by then) and dropped; a 404 ack is dropped as an empty poll — routine hand-off, not an error that escalates the backoff. A reference-shaped worker reading it as an ordinary error stays correct: its poller force-stops the unackable item **under the id it was handed** (`discardUnprocessable`), which the rotation neutralizes. The **first** hand-out keeps its enqueue-minted id (the one `lease_expires_at IS NULL` case), so the ordinary lifecycle is id-stable as the reference's; the row carries over unchanged (metadata, trace context, `created_at`), ids are opaque, and no wire field, status or shape changes. The `starting`/`active` reclaim also re-stamps `started_at`, by analogy with the reference's recorded same-id re-queue (*BetaSelfHostedWork — unreached lifecycle timestamps render null*); a lapsed un-acked reservation keeps it, never having left the queue. Two observable deltas, both of a kind any page over a live queue already carries: a client holding a lapsed hand-out's id must re-poll rather than reuse it; and because `ListWork` pages on a descending `(created_at, id)` keyset, an item that rotates between pages can be returned **twice** when its new id sorts below the cursor's (it ties itself on `created_at`, so no second row need tie), or skipped when its `created_at` ties and the new id sorts above. Whether the reference rotates is unrecorded — an earlier revision of this entry called the race a limitation the reference shares, withdrawn as unevidenced: the typed schema shows only that no proof is *exposed*, not what its reclaim does; ours does, deliberately. The cloud executor's `Claim` needs no equivalent — `Item.Lease` equality is already an ownership proof. *Evidence: internal/queue/queue.go Poll (`SET id = CASE WHEN t.lease_expires_at IS NULL …`; its comment holds the derivation cut here) and internal/worker/lease.go (pollAck, heartbeat, forceStop); internal/queue/lifecycle_test.go TestEveryReHandOutMintsAFreshWorkIdentity, internal/worker/lease_test.go TestStaleWorkerStopCannotStrandTheReplacement; no generation field, `{force}`-only stop body (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork and BetaSelfHostedWorkStopRequestParam) and discardUnprocessable (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go WorkPoller.discardUnprocessable); docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — the work-item lifecycle: get / ack / heartbeat / stop (slice 8, second part)" (the original wire-settled finding)* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference rotates, and what its reclaim does to `started_at`); landed for #62.*
- **traceparent to the BYOC worker — storage and transport** — The turn's trace context is stored in a dedicated trace_context jsonb column on work_items (migration 0005) — diverging from the plan's sketch of riding work-item metadata, because metadata is now client-updatable (PR C-meta) — and emitted as traceparent/tracestate response headers on /work/poll, never in the wire work body. The reference worker ignores unknown headers; ours reads them. Additive, wire-compatible surface. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — environment-key auth + `/work/poll` (slice 8, first part)", docs/history/2026-07.md § "BYOC worker — `traceparent` propagation across the process boundary (slice 8, PR C2b-2)" (PR C2b-2, 'Why a column, not metadata')*
- **GET /v1/environments/{id}/work/stats — provenance** — BetaSelfHostedWorkQueueStats is a derived view over Postgres (depth/pending/oldest_queued_at from the work_items snapshot; workers_polling from a poll-time worker-id record, migration 0006, 30-second window), not Redis Streams consumer-group figures. The SDK's field docs are Redis-native (depth = lag, pending = PEL, workers_polling = consumers), all but confirming the reference queue is Redis Streams; same wire numbers, Postgres provenance. Interpretation baked in: the wire's 'acknowledged' is our Ack (queued->starting), so depth = queued items available (no reservation or lapsed lease) and pending = polled-but-not-acked. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — the queue-stats endpoint (slice 8, PR C-stats)" ('Divergence noted: same wire numbers, Postgres provenance'); anthropic-sdk-go field docs cited there*
- **Helm chart (deploy/helm/managed-agent-platform) — plan-sketch divergences** — Postgres ships inline (StatefulSet, toggleable to externalDatabase.url) rather than as a subchart: an external chart-repo dependency undercuts air-gap self-hosting. The BYOC worker is intentionally out of the chart. **The gVisor RuntimeClass deferral is closed (#65):** the K8s provider now sets `runtimeClassName` on sandbox pods from `SANDBOX_K8S_RUNTIME_CLASS`, so the chart wires it as `sandboxRuntimeClass.name`, with an opt-in `sandboxRuntimeClass.create` that also creates the cluster-scoped RuntimeClass object (off by default — a cluster-scoped object collides between releases, needs cluster-scope RBAC to install, and needs the nodes already running the handler; it is Helm-managed, so `helm uninstall` removes it). *Evidence: internal/sandbox/k8s/k8s.go (podSpec's RuntimeClassName, runtimeClassName), internal/sandbox/k8s/hardening_test.go TestPodSpecRuntimeClass, deploy/helm/managed-agent-platform/templates/sandbox-runtimeclass.yaml; docs/history/2026-07.md § "Kubernetes sandbox provider (slice 9)"*

- **bash tool description — ours, and deliberately no longer the SDK's string** — The six sandbox tools' model-facing **schemas** are the wire's field for field; their **descriptions** are this platform's prose, and always have been (read/write/grep already differed from the SDK's client-side toolset text). `bash` was the one that still matched the SDK's `bashTool.Description()` verbatim, and no longer does: it now also tells the model to redirect a backgrounded process's output. The reason is behavioural rather than cosmetic — a backgrounded process inherits the exec's output stream and holds it open after the command itself exits, costing roughly two seconds per call while the daemon force-closes it (#65). What the reference's *server-side* toolset puts in front of the model is unobservable in every source we have, so matching the SDK's client-side string was never evidence of matching it; the schema, which is what a tool call is validated against, is untouched. *Evidence: internal/toolset/definitions.go (the bash entry and its comment); the SDK string this no longer matches (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — bash.go bashTool.Description), the descriptions that already differed (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — fs.go BetaReadTool and BetaWriteTool; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — search.go BetaGrepTool)*

- **Sandbox process limit — Docker only, because Kubernetes has no per-pod one** — `sandbox.Hardening.PidsLimit` (default 512) is applied by the Docker backend as `HostConfig.PidsLimit` and **ignored** by the Kubernetes backend: the Pod API defines no pids resource — a per-pod process cap is the kubelet's `podPidsLimit` node setting, not a pod-spec field (k8s.io/api v0.36.2 `core/v1` has no such `ResourceName`; a pod under a 500m CPU limit on kind reads `/sys/fs/cgroup/pids.max` as the node default, measured). The alternative — failing `Provision` on Kubernetes whenever a pids limit is configured — was rejected: the platform default is on, so it would break every Kubernetes deployment out of the box for a control the cluster expresses elsewhere. The asymmetry is therefore structural in the test suite rather than hidden: the shared contract's pids row is registered only for a backend declaring `Harness.EnforcesPidsLimit`, and `TestPodSpecIgnoresPidsLimit` pins the Kubernetes side as deliberate. Every other hardening dimension the suite can observe from inside a sandbox — CPU, capability drops, non-root, read-only rootfs — is asserted for both backends by the same shared rows; the memory cap is asserted at the provider level only, since observing it means being OOM-killed (plan 19 decision 6). Operators are pointed at `podPidsLimit` in docs/self-hosted-security.md §3. *Evidence: internal/sandbox/hardening.go (Hardening.PidsLimit doc), internal/sandbox/k8s/k8s.go (resourceRequirements), internal/sandbox/k8s/hardening_test.go TestPodSpecIgnoresPidsLimit, internal/sandbox/sandboxtest/contract.go (HardeningCapsProcesses registration), internal/sandbox/k8s/k8s_test.go (the harness comment)*
- **Sandbox disk cap — Kubernetes only, because Docker cannot enforce one honestly** — `sandbox.Hardening.EphemeralStorageBytes` (default 0, off) is applied by the Kubernetes backend as an `ephemeral-storage` limit *and* an equal request, and **ignored** by the Docker backend, which warns once per provider that the cap is unenforceable. Mirror of the pids row above, different cause: the Engine API *does* define `HostConfig.StorageOpt`, so this is not an absent field but one whose enforcement is conditional on the daemon's storage driver — some enforce it, some refuse the option outright, and Docker Desktop's `overlayfs` accepts it and enforces nothing at all (only the last is measured here; the rest are documented) — so the platform reports no cap it cannot vouch for. Failing `Provision` when it is set was rejected, the pids entry's reason inverted: this knob is off by default, so an operator who set it should be told it did nothing, not lose their executor. It is opt-in on Kubernetes for a sharper version of the memory cap's reason: a CPU or memory bound throttles or kills the process, this gets the whole **pod evicted** by the kubelet, taking the session's sandbox with it mid-call. Nor is the Kubernetes half unconditional: the kubelet applies the limit only on the node layouts whose local ephemeral storage it can measure, and on any other "does not apply resource limits for ephemeral local storage" — the pod accepts the fields and exceeds them unevicted (kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/storage/ephemeral-storage). Request equals limit deliberately — the kubelet ranks eviction candidates by usage against the *request*, so a limit the scheduler never reserved would land enforcement on an arbitrary pod rather than the one that filled the disk. No shared contract row: nothing inside a sandbox can observe this cap, so both backends assert it at the provider level (plan 19 decision 6, as for memory). *Evidence: internal/sandbox/docker/docker.go — the `warnUnenforceableEphemeralStorage` comment carries the storage-driver derivation and the measured no-op this row no longer restates — `docker run --rm --storage-opt size=1G alpine:3 df -h /` exits 0 and still reports the full host disk (docker 29.6.2/overlayfs, 2026-08-01); internal/sandbox/hardening.go (EphemeralStorageBytes doc); internal/sandbox/k8s/k8s.go resourceRequirements; internal/sandbox/k8s/ephemeral_test.go TestPodSpecAppliesEphemeralStorage; internal/sandbox/docker/ephemeral_test.go TestSandboxConfigIgnoresEphemeralStorage; internal/sandbox/sandboxtest/contract.go Harness; docs/self-hosted-security.md §3, the operator-facing version (the drivers by name, and the node layouts the kubelet can measure)* *Landed for docs/plan/20_gcp-deployment.md slice 2.*
- **K8s sandbox backend — deadline and limited-networking fidelity vs Docker** — Two documented necessity divergences from the Docker backend's guarantees: (1) the exec-deadline verdict reads state on the sandbox's own filesystem (Kubernetes has no out-of-band exec handle), so a malicious command can tamper with it in both directions — overwriting the pid file after killing its watchdog to hide an overrun, or writing the watchdog's kill mark and contriving a SIGKILL to claim a timeout it never hit (the mark is what classifies a punctual kill here, since the liveness probe is itself an exec and answers a round trip late — #95, #110). Out of scope for single-tenant v1: honest runaways forge nothing, every term of the verdict only ever *adds* a timeout so a forged mark cannot withdraw one, and a forged timeout mislabels only the tenant's own tool call. Docker keeps no such in-pod state — its probe is a daemon call the sandbox cannot reach — and deliberately still does not; (2) the limited-networking route flush is not equivalent to NetworkMode none on every cluster (raw AF_PACKET sockets can reach the segment; only the main IPv4 table is inspected, so policy-routing CNIs and IPv6 need the egress-gate sidecar — shipped as the plan-12 `executor.gateImage` opt-in, whose owner-match firewall replaces the flush on gated pods; the caveat still holds for deployments that leave the gate off). *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "Kubernetes sandbox provider (slice 9)"*

- **POST /v1/files — per-organization storage quota not enforced** — The public docs cap uploads at 500 MB per file (enforced, 413) *and* 500 GB per organization; the per-file cap is honored but the per-org quota is deliberately not — a self-hosted operator owns its own disk, and imposing a fixed 500 GB ceiling on a single-tenant deployment would be arbitrary. The per-file 500 MB cap and the documented filename validation (1–255 chars, no `<>:"|?*\/` or control chars) are enforced. *Evidence: docs/plan/08_files.md decision 4; platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/files ("File storage and limits"); internal/api/filesupload.go maxFileBytes* *Tracked: #55 (delivered; plan 08's Files API).*

- **Agent model effort — explicit selection honored; defaults and capabilities delegated to the configured endpoint (plan 53)** — The former silent drop is fixed: string and `{type: level}` inputs normalize to the response object for `low|medium|high|xhigh|max`; malformed selections return 400; agent updates omitting effort retain the saved level only when the model ID stays the same; a new ID resets to its own default. A session model override replaces the model config, while omitting the model inherits the pinned agent's selection. The [public agent guide](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/agent-setup) (read 2026-09-22) specifies the same-ID condition and says the reference ignores session-level effort; honoring an explicit session override here is a deliberate extension that avoids silently discarding the caller's selection. Primary, child and grader turns forward the level as Anthropic `output_config.effort` or OpenAI-compatible `reasoning_effort`. For backend defaults and capabilities, two deliberate differences remain: the reference resolves a per-model default at save time and always returns effort, whereas an unset platform effort stays absent and delegates to the upstream; the reference rejects unsupported model/effort pairs at creation, whereas this platform validates the level but leaves model-specific support to its configured endpoint. Client-facing model IDs may route to arbitrary upstream models, so a hard-coded Anthropic capability table would be misleading.
