An upstream rejection remains a visible model-request failure; protocol compatibility does not prove that an endpoint implements effort. *Evidence: checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsModelConfigParams.Effort and BetaManagedAgentsModelConfigEffortUnion; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — message.go OutputConfigParam.Effort; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentWithOverridesParams.Model; [OpenAI Chat Completions reasoning_effort](https://developers.openai.com/api/reference/resources/chat/subresources/completions/methods/create) (read 2026-09-22); internal/domain/agent.go, internal/api/agents.go, internal/brain/brain.go and grader.go, internal/provider/anthropic and openai. Landed for #160.*

- **Agent model `inference_geo` — protocol metadata, without geographic enforcement (plan 54)** — The user chose protocol compatibility for a deployment using operator-configured geography (2026-09-22). The optional string accepts `us` or `global`, persists through agent versions and session/roster snapshots, and echoes the caller's selection. Null means unset and is omitted from responses; invalid selections, including empty strings, return 400. Omitting the whole model preserves it; supplying a replacement model without the field clears it. Legacy snapshots omit the field. Unlike the reference, this metadata is not passed to either provider protocol and does not select a region. No workspace default/allowlist, per-model capability check or roster-wide matching-geo requirement is applied; actual inference geography is determined by the operator's deployment and upstream configuration, and a returned value is not proof of where inference ran. A supporting upstream could enforce a forwarded constraint, but the user explicitly excluded that behavior from scope. *Evidence: checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsModelConfigParams.InferenceGeo and BetaManagedAgentsModelConfig.InferenceGeo; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — message.go MessageNewParams.InferenceGeo; [agent guide](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/agent-setup#pin-the-inference-geo) and [session guide](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/sessions#pin-the-inference-geo-for-a-session), read 2026-09-22; internal/domain/agent.go; docs/plan/54_inference-geo-wire.md. Landed for #433.*


- **Work items — `secret` is the sessions token's envelope on the poll of a store session, null everywhere else (plan 36 slice 5, decision 15)** — `BetaSelfHostedWork` carries a required `secret` (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.59.0 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork.Secret), "may be populated when polling for work; null on all other retrieval paths", and the reference worker decodes it (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go sessionsTokenFromSecret): URL-safe base64, padded or not, of a JSON object whose `sessions_token` key becomes the Bearer for that item's lease heartbeat, force-stop, `GET /v1/sessions/{id}`, the session's events list/stream/send, the skill reads and every memory call, with the parent client's `X-Api-Key` deleted so the two credentials never ride together; an absent or undecodable payload logs a warning and falls back to the environment key, and a session with stores and no token **fails the whole work item** (`ErrSessionMemoryNoToken`). This platform renders `base64url(JSON {"sessions_token": "wtk_…"})` on the poll response of an item whose session attaches a memory store — minted per claim, hash-only at rest — and `null` on every other path and for every other item, so a storeless BYOC session is byte-for-byte what it was. The reference's envelope carries further keys the worker does not consume (`session_ingress_token`, `auth[]` in the SDK's fixture); they are absent here, and #165's vault-credential bundle would be one more key in the same envelope. What is confirmed is the envelope and the worker's use of it; whether the reference populates `secret` always or only for store sessions, and its other keys, are the INFERRED entry below. **This entry replaces one that read "`secret` rendered, always null"** — true of a worker that never read the field (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.63.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem) and false of one that does (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker.go sessionsTokenFromSecret) — rewritten rather than deleted so the reversal stays auditable. *Evidence: checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork.Secret; the decode, the per-item credential and the X-Api-Key delete, and the failure on stores without a token (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go sessionsTokenFromSecret and EnvironmentWorker.handleItem; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go helperReqOpts and bearerReqOpts; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go ErrSessionMemoryNoToken); no occurrence of "secret" in the worker, what the replaced entry rested on (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.63.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem); internal/worktoken (Secret, Mint), internal/api/workapi.go claimWork; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decisions 6 and 15* *Tracked: #165.*

- **Vault, agent, and session surfaces — documented limits enforced; environments document none (plan 12 D7; #66, #287, #289, #291, #665)** — The confirmed, deliberate divergence recorded here is the *asymmetry*: the vault/credential caps the public vaults guide documents are enforced on vaults, the agent caps the SDK's create/update params document are enforced on agents (#66), the whole-spec ones (tools and mcp_servers) on every session's resolved agent too — create-time resolve and the update patch's merged result alike (#287) — and the session metadata caps the same SDK bounds with the identical sentence ("Maximum 16 pairs, keys up to 64 chars, values up to 512 chars", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaSessionNewParams.Metadata) are enforced on session create and update (#289; the SDK documents them on the create params only — its session *update* params are silent, unlike agents' which restate the bound, so binding the post-patch bag extends the create-time doc rather than quoting an update-param one) — while **environments** enforce nothing because nothing documents caps for them; recorded once rather than silently harmonized in any direction. The vault caps: metadata ≤16 pairs / keys ≤64 / values ≤512 (create and after every patch — the same numbers the SDK documents for agent and session metadata, so the check is one shared helper), vault display_name required 1–255, credential display_name ≤255, ≤16 allowed_hosts, ≤20 credentials per vault. The agent caps: ≤128 tools, ≤20 mcp_servers with unique names and every server referenced by an `mcp_toolset` in the resulting tools, and the shared metadata caps; beside them `name` ≤256, `description` ≤2,048 and `system` ≤100,000 characters, which bind only a value a create or update supplies — never a stored one, and so never at resolve (#665; the spec's `maxLength`, which the Go doc comments drop — the agent-caps entry under INFERRED). The shared metadata caps are not sessions' only documented bounds: the create-time `initial_events` rules the public sessions page documents (50 events, 100 file-sourced document blocks, one define_outcome — the SDK states only the first, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaSessionNewParams.InitialEvents) are enforced too — recorded in the define_outcome entry below — and the 100,000-character ceiling on an `agent_with_overrides` replacement `system` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentWithOverridesParams.System) is enforced as well (#291) — the entry after this one records both `system` bounds' recorded rejects. Enforcement is our own — the *reference's* exact reject statuses and strings for exceeding a cap are, beyond those `system` rejects, unobserved and recorded under INFERRED below (the first vault entry, and the agent-caps entry, which covers the session metadata rejects too — same helper, same messages); this entry claims only that we enforce them and that environments do not. *Evidence: platform.claude.com vaults guide (limits table); platform.claude.com managed-agents/sessions (fetched 2026-09-24 — "accepts a maximum of 50 events", and the create-request rejection table's rows for more than one `user.define_outcome` event and more than 100 file-sourced `document` content blocks across the whole list); the create/update param doc comments (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaAgentNewParams and BetaAgentUpdateParams; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaSessionNewParams and BetaSessionUpdateParams and BetaManagedAgentsAgentWithOverridesParams.System); internal/api/wire.go (validateMetadataCaps, validateAgentSpec); internal/api/sessions.go parseInitialEvents; internal/api/vaults_test.go TestVaultValidation; internal/api/agentlimits_test.go; internal/api/sessions_test.go TestSessionMetadataCaps* *Tracked: #50 (delivered; the vault caps are plan 12's, the agent and session caps the issues named above).*
- **`system` past 100,000 characters on agent create and on `agent_with_overrides` — the recorded rejects' status and type match, and the override's code-point unit; only the message is ours (confirmed 2026-09-02; #291, #665)** — The override's bound is its doc comment's ("Up to 100,000 characters", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentWithOverridesParams.System); agent create's is the spec's `maxLength` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCreateAgentParams.properties.system), which the Go doc comment drops — why the override's landed for #291 and the agent's not until #665. **Recorded, and matched here:** agent create refused 100,001 ASCII characters with 400 `invalid_request_error` and accepted 100,000 `é` (200,000 UTF-8 bytes), which rules out bytes but cannot tell code points from UTF-16 units, `é` being one of each; the override refused 100,001 ASCII characters with 400 `invalid_request_error` and accepted 50,001 astral characters (100,002 UTF-16 units), so the override counts **code points**, as `utf8.RuneCountInString` does. **Ours:** the messages — the reference's two validators do not even agree with each other (`system: maximum string length is 100000` on agent create, `agent.agent_with_overrides.config.system: must be at most 100000 characters` on the override), and a client branches on the type, never the prose; and that the override check binds only what the override supplies, never counting `system:null` (clear) or a preserved stored value. What stays inferred on the agent side — its unit past the BMP, update, stored values — is the agent-caps entry's under INFERRED. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/batch2.json, probes resolved by name — `agent.create.system-100001-ascii` (400), `agent.create.system-100000-eacute` (200), `session.create.overrides.system-100001-ascii` (400), `session.create.overrides.system-100000-eacute` (200), `session.create.overrides.system-50001-astral` (200).* *Evidence: internal/api/wire.go (maxAgentSystemRunes, capRunes); internal/api/agents.go (parseAgentSpecFields); internal/api/sessions.go (overrideSystemMaxRunes, resolveAgent); internal/api/agentlimits_test.go TestAgentSystemCap; internal/api/sessions_test.go TestSessionOverrideSystemCap*
- **Vault placeholders — `vltph_` opaque tokens, ours to mint (plan 12 D4)** — The sandbox-visible stand-in for an environment-variable vault secret is an "opaque placeholder" the reference defines no format for; we use `vltph_`-prefixed tokens (the prefix plus 128 bits of hex). Both the prefix and the format are ours (deliberate). The token is **derived deterministically per `(session, secret_name)`** (SHA-256), not minted at random: `sandbox.Spec.Env` is bound at container create and kept across a session's idempotent re-provisions, so every executor pass and the egress gate must recover the exact token already in the sandbox rather than drift to a fresh value the gate could no longer substitute; a rotated credential keeps its placeholder. Determinism is safe because the placeholder is opaque, not a secret — the per-session gate substitutes only a session's own placeholders and only for a host the credential's `allowed_hosts` admit, so the security boundary is the gate and the host allow-list, never placeholder secrecy. Placeholder minting, injection into `Spec.Env`, and egress-time substitution are all live: the per-session gate substitutes a session's own placeholders on admitted plain-HTTP egress on both backends (Docker gate-pair, K8s native sidecar), leaving in-sandbox HTTPS placeholders literal until the #166 TLS gap closes. This never affects wire compatibility: a placeholder never crosses the management API — it lives only inside the sandbox and the egress path — so the reference's own placeholder shape is unobserved and irrelevant. *Evidence: internal/egress/engine.go (PlaceholderPrefix, Placeholder); internal/egress/engine_test.go TestPlaceholder; internal/vaultresolve/vaultresolve.go; plan 12 D4* *Tracked: #50 (delivered; in-sandbox HTTPS substitution of the placeholder is the open #166).*
- **Internal gate-config endpoint (`GET /internal/v1/gate/config`) (plan 12 slice 4)** — A control-plane endpoint **outside the public `/v1` wire**, authenticated by a per-session `gtk_` gate token (`Authorization: Bearer`) on its own lane in `dispatchAuth` — selected by path, so a `gtk_` token never reaches a management handler and an `x-api-key` never reaches this one. It returns the session's environment networking policy and its resolved, **decrypted** vault credentials (placeholder, secret, the credential's own `allowed_hosts` arm, injection locations, and non-secret `credential_id`) — the two inputs `internal/gate` needs. The endpoint and its client (`internal/gateconfig`) are entirely ours: the reference's gate is a managed black box, so a self-hosting gate that fetches its own policy and secrets has no reference equivalent and observes no reference wire shape — a settled, reference-less deliberate divergence (nothing here awaits a recording). Plaintext secrets cross this internal boundary in the response body only; the resolution error carries credential ids, never secret or ciphertext bytes (`vaultresolve.Credentials`). A 401 (revoked token / archived session) is the gate's unambiguous fail-closed signal; a transient error leaves it on last-known-good. *Evidence: internal/api/gateauth.go (requireGateToken, isGateConfigPath), internal/api/gateconfig.go (getGateConfig), internal/gateconfig (Client, Config, ErrUnauthorized); internal/gatetoken; plan 12 slice 4* *Tracked: #50 (delivered; the endpoint shipped with plan 12's gate and serves only it).*

- **Vault credential create, update and `mcp_oauth_validate` (deployment-configured) — a `gcpkms` deployment bounds secret material at the key's ceiling: 64 KiB, or 8 KiB on an HSM key** — With `SECRETS_BACKEND=gcpkms`, credential secret material whose sealed plaintext — the JSON object built from all of a credential's secret fields, so the bound is on their sum — exceeds that ceiling is refused with a **400 `invalid_request_error` naming the limit**, where the identical request on the identical build succeeds under the `local` or `openbao` cipher; which of the two ceilings binds is a property of the key version, not of the code, and a resource name does not reveal it. Three request paths answer this way: create (`POST /v1/vaults/{id}/credentials`) and update (`POST …/credentials/{cid}`), where the oversize material came from the request body, and `POST …/credentials/{cid}/mcp_oauth_validate`, where it came from the token endpoint's refresh response rather than from the caller at all — a 4xx is an imperfect fit there and is used anyway, so its message says where the material came from instead of implying the request carried it (`resealFailed`'s comment holds that argument). No other request path reaches the ceiling: the only other seal on one, a `github_repository` resource's `authorization_token`, is capped by the API at 8 KiB first. So this is a property of one cipher rather than of the wire — the API bounds the length of no credential secret field and admits a 4 MiB body (`maxBodyBytes`) — and the 4xx is deliberate: an unclassified `Cipher.Encrypt` failure renders as a generic 500 whose useful text reaches only the server's log, so the backend returns `secrets.ErrPlaintextTooLarge` and the vault-credential handler classifies it into the `errInvalid` family. Envelope encryption would remove the ceiling and is deferred. *Evidence: internal/secrets/gcpkms/gcpkms.go — its package doc and the MaxPlaintextBytes/MaxPlaintextBytesHSM comments carry the measured ceilings and the fail-closed protection-level derivation this entry no longer restates; internal/secrets/secrets.go (ErrPlaintextTooLarge); internal/api/vaultcredentials.go (sealFailed, resealFailed); docs/plan/20_gcp-deployment.md Decision 3 and its 2026-08-01 ground-truth probes; gcpkms TestTheCeilingIsExactAndClassified / TestAnHSMKeyGetsTheSmallerCeiling / TestEveryProtectionLevelResolvesItsCeiling, api TestOversizeCredentialSecretIsRefusedWithAFourHundred / TestAnOversizeRefreshedTokenIsRefusedWithAFourHundred* *Tracked: #242.*

- **The deliverables harvest on `self_hosted` environments — never, on either trigger (plan 21 slice 4, plan 38)** — A cloud session snapshots `/mnt/session/outputs/` into the files registry twice over: when a grading cycle begins, and whenever the session folds idle. A self_hosted session does neither — its grading settlement schedules the grading turn directly and the grader judges from the transcript (plus any rubric) alone, and its idle folds schedule nothing at all, so `GET /v1/files?scope_id=` stays empty for the life of the session. The exclusion lives at all six enqueue sites (the grading branch and the five idle folds), never in the queue's claim predicate. Deliberate: the sandbox lives on customer compute the platform cannot reach, the wire work API serves only `tool_exec` (a harvest kind would be a new wire surface the reference does not have), and shipping deliverable bytes back over the worker wire is a data-boundary decision the customer has not made. The reference is cloud-only on this axis, so there is no reference behavior to diverge from — the divergence is that the *same platform* grades the two environment kinds from different evidence. A future opt-in worker-side harvest could close the gap. *Evidence: internal/brain/grader.go (settleEndTurn's environment-kind routing, and enqueueIdleHarvest's identical guard shared by the five idle folds); internal/queue/queue.go (Poll's tool_exec-only scope); docs/plan/21_outcomes.md Decision 8; docs/plan/38_idle-outputs-harvest.md decisions 4 and 6; internal/brain/grader_test.go TestEndTurnOnSelfHostedNeverHarvests, internal/brain/brain_test.go TestPermissionAskIdleEnqueuesOutputsHarvest; BetaSelfHostedWork is session-scoped work carrying a state enum, with no per-tool or outputs_harvest work kind, so no harvest is ever routed to a BYOC worker (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork and BetaSelfHostedWorkState)* *Tracked: #77 (delivered).*

- **`github_repository` resources on `self_hosted` environments — nothing is materialized, and the agent is not told otherwise (plan 25 slice 2)** — A repository attached to a cloud session is cloned into its sandbox by the executor; the same resource on a self_hosted session is materialized by nothing, and the brain omits the "Mounted repositories" block there rather than describing a checkout that does not exist. This half is **CONFIRMED against the reference**, though not by a sentence in the docs — the public GitHub guide describes mounting a repository "to your session sandbox" and never mentions environment kinds at all. It is confirmed by what the reference *ships*: the only component that can write into a customer-hosted sandbox is the customer-run worker, and the reference's worker is a container ENTRYPOINT that executes `agent.tool_use` events and contains no repository, git, or clone handling whatsoever; nor is it given anything to clone with, since a self-hosted work item's data is `{id, type:"session"}` — it names a session and carries no materialization payload and no credential. A repository therefore cannot be materialized on self_hosted by the reference either, and the platform's silence matches. What is *ours* is the shape of the gap on a self-hosting deployment: the resource is accepted, sealed, listed, and rotatable on a self_hosted session, and simply never appears in the sandbox, with no event saying so. Deliberate for v1 rather than an oversight — a BYOC clone means shipping the customer's token to customer compute, or teaching the worker wire a materialization kind, and neither is a decision this plan makes. The brain gate is what keeps the omission honest: a block naming `/workspace/repo` to a model whose sandbox has no such path is worse than no block at all. *Evidence: docs/plan/25_git-repo-mounting.md decisions 8-9 (whose "CONFIRMED-by-docs" shorthand is narrowed here — the guide is silent, the shipped client is not); platform.claude.com managed-agents/github (fetched 2026-08-07 — no mention of environment kinds); BetaSessionWorkData = `{id, type:"session"}` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSessionWorkData); anthropic-cli's worker command ("Attach to a session and execute agent.tool_use events locally. Intended as a container ENTRYPOINT." — no repository handling anywhere in pkg/cmd/worker.go; checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — worker.go workerRunCommand); internal/brain/repos.go (resolveReposBlock's environment-kind gate); internal/executor/repos.go (materializeRepos, reached only by the platform-managed executor)* *Tracked: #322 (the session half — a repository accepted, sealed and rotatable on a self_hosted session that nothing ever materializes).*

- **Session update (`POST /v1/sessions/{id}`) — the documented idle requirement is not enforced** — The reference docs state a session "must be in an `idle` state" to be updated; our update handler applies the change regardless of status — a `running` or `rescheduling` session's title/metadata update succeeds where the reference documents a refusal. A known pre-existing gap, registered when plan 24 slice 1 added the running-session archive/delete guard next door: the update predicate is *stricter* than running-only (idle-only would also refuse `rescheduling`, `terminated`…), so it is not the same guard and needs its own decision — enforce idle-only, running-only, or leave open — plus the refusal's unobserved wire shape. Registered, not fixed, in that slice. Unlike its neighbors this entry is **not an accepted design**: it sits here so the wire-compat rung sees the mismatch flagged rather than silently absent, and #312 owns the decision — a reviewer must not suppress an update-while-running finding on this entry's authority. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/session-operations ("Updating session properties", fetched 2026-08-05); internal/api/sessions.go (updateSession — no status check)* *Tracked: #312.*

- **Human authentication (`/v1` management paths and the `/api/` console namespace) — the deployment IdP's JWTs are accepted as `Authorization: Bearer`, and no `POST /v1/oauth/token` is served (plan 31 slice 2)** — The reference API host serves `POST /v1/oauth/token` (authorization_code + refresh_token + jwt-bearer) and its console authenticates humans with the tokens that endpoint mints; this platform serves no such endpoint, so `ant auth login`, OAuth profiles, and OIDC federation against it are unsupported. In their place, a deployment that configures `IDENTITY_MODE` accepts a JWT issued by **its own** IdP — verified against that issuer's JWKS, never minted here — on the same management paths. Neither surface is on the wire any documented CLI or SDK flow uses: management rides `x-api-key` and workers ride environment keys, and both are unchanged, byte for byte, whether identity is configured or not — with one deliberate exception in every mode: a *repeated* `x-api-key` field, which HTTP permits and no documented client sends, is now refused as ambiguous rather than resolved by header order. The `x-api-key` lane wins outright when a key is present, so a Bearer can never vouch for a management call. With `IDENTITY_MODE` unset or `disabled` — the default — no JWT is accepted anywhere and a Bearer on a management path draws the same missing-key 401 it always has. *Evidence: `ant auth login` and its two token grants (checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — cmd_auth.go authLogin and exchangeCode and refreshAccessToken), the reference's token endpoint and grant types (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — federation.go TokenEndpoint and GrantTypeJWTBearer and GrantTypeRefreshToken); internal/api/server.go (dispatchManagementAuth: x-api-key first, human second, today's 401 when neither), internal/api/identitylane.go (identityCredential), internal/identity/verifier.go (Verify); internal/api/identitylane_test.go (TestIdentityDisabledIsUnchanged, TestManagementKeyWinsOverAHumanCredential); docs/plan/31_console-sso-rbac.md* *Landed for #56's SSO/RBAC half (plan 31 slice 2).*
- **Console roles — three (`admin`/`developer`/`viewer`), bound to IdP claims rather than a platform user store (plan 31 slice 2)** — The reference console has seven roles over its own account/organization membership. This platform has three, ordered `viewer < developer < admin`, and holds **no** authority of its own: a request's role is computed per request from the claim the deployment's IdP asserts, through the operator's `IDENTITY_ROLE_MAP`. The `principals` table records who has signed in (issuer, subject, email, display name) for audit and for `created_by`, and deliberately carries no role column — a stored copy would be a second, staler answer to a question the IdP already answers, and revoking a group at the IdP must take effect on the next request rather than after an operator remembers to mirror it here. A token that verifies but maps to no role is authenticated with no authority and is refused by every role-gated route. *Evidence: internal/identity/identity.go (Role, roleRank, AtLeast), internal/identity/claims.go (strongestRole), internal/store/migrations/0022_principals.sql (the no-role-column argument), internal/api/identitylane.go (requireRole); internal/api/identitylane_test.go (the role-column assertion, the unmapped-role 403); docs/plan/31_console-sso-rbac.md* *Landed for #56's SSO/RBAC half (plan 31 slice 2).*
- **`IDENTITY_MODE=trusted_proxy` — a mode with no reference analog (plan 31 slice 2)** — The reference is a hosted service and is never deployed behind a customer's identity-aware proxy; this platform is meant to be, so it accepts a proxy's signed assertion header (the shipped `gcp-iap` preset reads `x-goog-iap-jwt-assertion` against Google's global key set) as the human credential. The assertion is verified exactly as an OIDC token is — same allowlist, same issuer/audience/expiry discipline — never trusted because it arrived from a proxy. In this mode `Authorization: Bearer` is **never** read as a human credential, so a caller cannot present a token the proxy never vouched for; Bearer stays what it already was, the worker's environment key. The rule is closed from the configuration end too: naming `Authorization` or `x-api-key` as the assertion header is a **startup** error, since either would invert the rule from the other side — the API layer would read a raw `Authorization` value as an assertion and hand a worker's environment key to the verifier. *Evidence: internal/identity/preset.go (gcpIAPPreset), internal/identity/config.go (configureGCPIAP, configureCustomProxy), internal/api/identitylane.go (identityCredential's trusted_proxy branch); internal/api/identitylane_test.go (TestTrustedProxyModeReadsOnlyItsHeader); docs/plan/31_console-sso-rbac.md* *Landed for #56's SSO/RBAC half (plan 31 slice 2).*

- **No `/v1/organizations/api_keys` — creation withheld as the reference withholds it, read/update omitted as Admin-API surface (plan 32 slice 2)** — Management keys are issued, listed and updated only on the console namespace, at `/api/console/organizations/{org}/workspaces/{workspace}/api_keys`. Two different absences are folded into one line here, and they have different characters. **Creation** has no public endpoint on the reference either, deliberately and for the stated reason — its Admin API FAQ reads "new API keys can only be created through the Claude Console for security reasons", and its API reference lists only Retrieve, List and Update — so withholding it is *mirroring*, the same move plan 30 made for environment-key issuance. **Read and update** do exist publicly, as `GET /v1/organizations/api_keys` and `POST /v1/organizations/api_keys/{id}`, and we do not serve them: they belong to the Console Admin API, authenticated by an `sk-ant-admin…` credential class this platform has no analog of, and outside the Managed Agents wire-compat target this project scopes itself to. A consequence worth stating plainly, because it is the shape a client would notice: the same resource has three collection shapes on the reference — the console's **bare array**, the Admin API's `{data, first_id, has_more, last_id}`, and this platform's own `{data, next_page}` wire envelope — and we implement only the first, on the only surface that carries it. *Evidence: the Admin API FAQ and reference index; the live recording of 2026-08-13 in [#378](https://github.com/OpenSDLC-Dev/managed-agent-platform/issues/378), where `GET …/api_keys` returned a bare array.*
- **Client-supplied `expires_at` on management keys, against a server-fixed TTL on environment keys (plan 32 slice 2)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#389).** The one mismatch this entry records is a refusal this platform has since lifted. `POST …/api_keys` takes an optional absolute ISO-8601 instant and stores it verbatim; **absent means never**, which is what the reference's own console sends for its *Never* choice — it omits the field rather than nulling it, and the response then reports `expires_at: null`. There is no duration vocabulary on the wire at all: the dialog's "3 hours / 30 days / Custom *N* units" is client-side sugar resolved to an instant before sending. This is a deliberate asymmetry with plan 30's environment keys, whose lifetime is the platform's fixed `EnvironmentKeyTTL` and not the caller's to choose, and it is the reference's own asymmetry: an operator issuing a management credential picks its lifetime, while a worker credential's lifetime is infrastructure. An instant already in the past is **accepted**, and mints a key already reporting `expired`: this platform refused it until [#389](https://github.com/OpenSDLC-Dev/managed-agent-platform/issues/389) probed the reference, which answers 200 to an `expires_at` of 2020. The refusal had been the safer-looking guess — a validation message beats debugging an authentication failure — and it lost to the rule that governs here. **The `name` bound** is the environment-key surface's, reused rather than re-chosen: 1–128 characters after trimming, with a whitespace-only name rejected. This dialect's own bound is unobserved — the recording never probed one — so 128 is a local pick sized to a hostname-or-phrase label, exactly as plan 30's entry says of the same number on the other console surface. *Evidence: the `Never` capture and the `{"name":…,"expires_at":…}` capture, both in [#378](https://github.com/OpenSDLC-Dev/managed-agent-platform/issues/378); `consoleKeyName` in `internal/api/consoleapikeys.go`.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the management-key dialect's own `name` bound, which the 2026-08-13 console recording never probed and this entry fills with the environment-key surface's 1–128); landed for #389.*
- **`can_manage` omitted from the listing while the surface stays admin-only (plan 32 slice 2)** — The reference's console rows carry a `can_manage` boolean the created resource does not: a **per-row authorization hint**, the server telling the console whether *this viewer* may act on *this row*, rather than the console deriving it from a role. Every row in the recording returned `true`, because that account's user is an admin; its falsy case is unobserved. We omit the field. These routes are gated whole at `admin` (plan 31's role mechanism), so the value would be a constant `true` — a column reserved for a permission split this platform has not built, and one whose only honest value today carries no information. It is worth keeping in view rather than merely dropping: it is the reference's answer to "how does a console grey out the buttons it must not offer", which is a question our own RBAC will eventually have to answer too. *Evidence: the row-shape capture in [#378](https://github.com/OpenSDLC-Dev/managed-agent-platform/issues/378).* *Tracked: #78 (a recording made from a non-admin console account would settle `can_manage`'s falsy case, which an admin account cannot show).*
- **`sk-map-api01-` as the issued-key prefix, and `principal`/`api_key` actors where the reference says `user` (plan 32 slice 2)** — Issued management keys read `sk-map-api01-` plus 43 base64url characters, beside plan 30's `sk-map-env01-` and against the reference's `sk-ant-api03-`. The silhouette is modeled on it; the middle is deliberately ours, for the reason plan 30 gives: a credential that reads like an Anthropic key invites being pasted into `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, and nothing parses the prefix — to every consumer the key is an opaque `x-api-key` value. The resource's actor fields diverge for a harder reason: the reference renders `created_by` and `principal` as `{"id": "user_…", "type": "user"}`, and this platform has no `user_` id to give, because sessions and credentials here are deliberately not bound to an end user (design principle 5). `created_by` therefore renders `{"id": "principal_…", "type": "principal"}` for a human who issued the key over SSO, `{"id": "apikey_…", "type": "api_key"}` for a machine credential that did, and `null` for the key seeded from `CONTROLPLANE_API_KEY` — which nobody issued. `workspace_id` and `principal` are emitted `null`, which is mirroring rather than reserving-by-guess: both came back null on the reference's own single-tenant account, so [#56](https://github.com/OpenSDLC-Dev/managed-agent-platform/issues/56) can populate them without a shape change. *Evidence: the created-resource capture in [#378](https://github.com/OpenSDLC-Dev/managed-agent-platform/issues/378); `internal/api/consoleapikeys.go`.*
- **A key seeded from `CONTROLPLANE_API_KEY` is listed but not console-mutable (plan 32 slice 2)** — The console lists every management key, including the one the environment variable seeds — hiding the credential the caller is authenticating with would misdescribe what can reach this API — but `POST …/api_keys/{id}` refuses to change its status or its name, naming the variable in the 400. The reference has no analog to diverge from: it has no env-var-managed credential class. The rule is ours and it is structural. That row's lifecycle already has an owner, rotation-by-restart, so a console disable would be silently undone by the next boot; and renaming it would break that rotation outright, since `EnsureAPIKey` archives the incumbent **by name** — a bootstrap row renamed out from under it would survive the next rotation and leave two live credentials under one name, which is precisely the race `api_keys_one_live_unissued` exists to prevent. *Evidence: `internal/api/consoleapikeys.go`'s `updateAPIKey`; `TestAPIKeyUpdateGuardsTheEnumAndTheEnvManagedRow`, whose negative control observes the control plane's own key 401 once the guard is removed.*

- **The lifecycle refusals say the same thing in different words (plan 32 slice 2, measured by [#389](https://github.com/OpenSDLC-Dev/managed-agent-platform/issues/389))** — The two state guards on `POST …/api_keys/{id}` now match the reference's *behaviour* exactly, and deliberately not its *wording*. Where the reference answers `"Archived API keys cannot be updated."` and `"Expired API keys can only be deleted, not renamed or reactivated."`, this platform answers `"api key <id> is archived, and an archived key cannot be updated"` and `"api key <id> has expired; an expired key can only be archived, not renamed or re-activated"`. Three reasons to keep ours: the id names *which* key of several a caller may have patched in a batch; "archived" is the remedy an operator can act on here, where the reference's "deleted" is a UI verb for a state this API spells `archived` and serves no DELETE for; and every other refusal on this surface already names its subject, so adopting the reference's phrasing on two of them alone would make the surface less consistent, not more. The enclosing envelope and the HTTP status **are** mirrored (`400`, `{"type":"error","error":{"type":"invalid_request_error","message":…},"request_id":…}` — verified identical in the probe), and no client can match on message text without breaking against the reference's own copy edits. Registered because the strings are now *observed* rather than unknown: before #389 this was an unrecorded gap, of the kind plan 30's own entry above describes for the console dialect's edges. *Evidence: `internal/api/consoleapikeys.go:285` and `:301`; the measured strings in [docs/HISTORY.md](HISTORY.md)'s #389 probe record.*
- **The dial-address floor — what a customer-URL connection may reach (plan 29 slice 2)** — Five production paths resolve a host a customer or an agent named, and all five go through `internal/dialguard` (plan 44): the vault-credential validate probe, the MCP client's `DefaultClient` (which the executor's discovery driver selects by supplying no client of its own), the executor's dial-time token-endpoint refresh, which redials long after the probe last vetted the address, the JWKS fetch, and the per-session egress gate. The guard refuses loopback, link-local (cloud metadata), the unspecified address and multicast on the **resolved** IP of every dial, and re-checks the embedded IPv4 target inside six specification-fixed IPv6 transition forms; an address it cannot read at all is refused rather than admitted, so the natural `IPAllowed(net.ParseIP(host))` call shape cannot fail open. **None of this is reference behavior**: the reference documents reachability but no per-address policy, and a refusal on its side would be indistinguishable from an ordinary `mcp_connection_failed_error` — so both the list and its consequence are ours. **RFC 1918 is deliberately allowed**, the reading most likely to look like an omission: this platform is self-hosted by premise, so an MCP server on the operator's own private network is the normal case, and this guard is the floor beneath an agent's egress policy rather than the policy itself. One neighbouring dial is deliberately *not* behind it, and reading the guard as universal misplaces it: the `github_repository` clone is pinned to the literal `github.com` by a create-time grammar rather than an address check. Inside the gate the floor is per admission class rather than per dial, and after plan 45 exactly one class is exempt — a host in the operator's own `allowed_hosts`, where naming a private address *is* the vouching. `unrestricted` is no longer exempt (#570), which is the reference's own answer; what remains outside the floor entirely is a session provisioned no gate at all (#620). The decoding is hardening on the one prefix an attacker can count on being routed, not a general answer to NAT64: a Network-Specific Prefix carries nothing that marks it as one, so a deployment translating through its own space gets no decoding, and the over-refusal this costs is real, ranges from 0% to total depending on the deployment's prefix bytes rather than only its length, and is fail-closed by choice. *Evidence: internal/dialguard/dialguard.go, whose comments carry the whole derivation this entry used to duplicate — which six forms are decoded and why, the RFC 6052 six-candidate read and the padding skip that makes it usable, the RFC 8215 §5 sentence that argues against the decoding and why a guard deciding a refusal is not a node forwarding a packet, the measured over-refusal figures, and the RFC 2529 6over4 omission taken by decision rather than oversight; internal/dialguard/dialguard_test.go pins each class and form; internal/api/vaultvalidate.go (the overridable probe seam), internal/mcp/mcp.go (DefaultClient), internal/executor/mcpwork.go (the driver that selects it).* *Landed for #45.*
- **The session delegation bound — what a session may spend when nobody is asking (#447)** — **OURS, and not an implementation of anything: the reference publishes no bound of any kind on autonomous agent-to-agent activity.** A session counts, in `sessions.delegation_turns`, every turn that called at least one delegation tool its own role was actually offered (a call to the other half's tool, or to a name the agent was never offered, is answered without counting — #567), and a claim is refused once that count reaches **625** (`maxLiveThreads × maxSettlementChain` — what every thread the session may hold could each spend under the thread-local chain cap; a 24-child coordinator over ten rounds is roughly 480 counted turns, so a rounder-looking figure would cut legitimate work). Only a demand from outside resets it — `user.message`, `user.define_outcome`, `user.tool_confirmation`, `system.message` (`domain.EventType.StartsNewWork`, deliberately narrower than `Inbound`). A tool result does **not**: on a `self_hosted` environment every tool call arrives as a client POST, so resetting there would zero the counter continuously and the bound would not exist. Nor does `user.interrupt` — a stop is not a demand, and counting it would let an operator's own stop hand the session a fresh budget. Counting is not restricted to settlement-only turns, or a model pairing one trivial tool call with each message would never move it. The refusal is at the **claim**, not the settlement: it never intervenes in a turn's fate, so it cannot idle a thread whose `tool_exec` is still in flight, and it needs no gate on `send_to_agent`, whose pinned `Message sent.` therefore stays true — the peer really is woken, and its own next claim is what is refused. A refused turn issues **no model request**: the thread idles `end_turn` carrying a `session.error` of the platform-coined type `session_delegation_exhausted_error` with `retry_status: exhausted`, a refused child delivers its coordinator the ending notice every other ending owes it, and overshoot is bounded at one model request per thread already in flight. **Why not the reference's `budget`**: it is a monetary ceiling (`max_list_cost`, a `BetaMonetaryAmount` in minor units of an ISO-4217 currency, under a discriminator whose only value is `"limit"`), and its documented recovery is to **raise the budget** — a remedy that presupposes a price to raise, where this bound's remedy is a client message. Implementing it needs a per-model list-price table that a gateway or self-hosted model cannot have (design principle 4), so a budget clone degrades to no bound at all for the deployments this platform exists for. This is therefore **not** a partial implementation of #432: `budget` still renders `null`, `session.usage` is still never emitted, `budget_reached` is still never written, and `domain.StopReasonType` is untouched. What it does **not** bound, so that nobody reads it as a runaway guard: a single agent looping `bash` forever off one client message. A pair emitting one real tool call alongside each message is bounded on a `cloud` environment but **not** on a `self_hosted` one, and the difference is which event answers the call — an executor posts `agent.tool_result`, which is stamped at write and is not pending input, so the refusal fires; a `self_hosted` client POSTs `user.tool_result`, which is, so the refusal declines rather than strand the thread. A client-executed custom tool takes the `self_hosted` lane on either kind. The cut is session-wide, so sibling threads doing real work stop beside the looping pair; and the counter is a projection no fold rebuilds (the `sessions.usage` precedent), trustworthy because exactly two statements write it and both hold the session row lock. *Evidence: `budget` is "A hard spend ceiling" on "tracked list cost" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsBudgetLimit), "Raise the budget to continue" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsSessionBudgetReached), the idle stop-reason union closed at four values, none an activity-exhaustion state (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsSessionStatusIdleEventStopReasonUnion.Type and BetaManagedAgentsSessionThreadStatusIdleEventStopReasonUnion.Type); platform.claude.com managed-agents multiagent-orchestration (structural limits only — roster 1–20, depth 1, 25 concurrent threads — and the roster entry having "no `max_uses`, `max_tokens`, or `caching` fields", a mechanism deliberately removed); internal/store/migrations/0027_session_delegation_turns.sql; internal/domain/event.go (StartsNewWork); internal/events/log.go (the reset inside AppendInTx); internal/brain/delegate.go (maxSessionDelegationTurns, runExhausted, cutExhaustedRun); TestEventStartsNewWork, TestSessionDelegationBudgetIsDerived, TestADelegatedTurnSpendsFromTheSessionBudget, TestAMixedDelegationAndToolTurnSpendsToo, TestAChildCallingTheCoordinatorsToolAlongsideBashNeverSpendsTheSessionBudget, TestAChildCallingTheCoordinatorsToolAloneHitsTheChainCapNeverTheSessionBudget, TestAClientMessageReturnsTheDelegationBudget, TestAToolResultDoesNotReturnTheDelegationBudget, TestTheClaimIsRefusedAtTheDelegationBound, TestAPeerWakeClearsTheChainCountButNotTheSessionBudget, TestPendingInputRunsTheTurnInsteadOfRefusing, TestARefusedChildTellsItsCoordinatorAndTheCascadeStops* *Landed for #447.*
- **Memory-version `created_by` / `redacted_by` — `service_account_actor` is never emitted (plan 36 slice 0; the rows land with slice 2)** — The reference's actor union is a closed set of four: `session_actor {session_id}` for an agent's write through the mounted filesystem, `api_actor {api_key_id}` for a write outside any session, `user_actor {user_id}` for the Anthropic Console, and — added inside this bump's range (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.64.0 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaManagedAgentsServiceAccountActor) — `service_account_actor {service_account_id}`, "a write made by a workload authenticated as a service account, for example via Workload Identity Federation". This platform has no service-account identity to name: `internal/identity` maps OIDC or trusted-proxy claims to three console roles, and no `svac_`-shaped id exists anywhere in it. Its memory versions will therefore render three of the union's four arms and never the fourth, and the `service_account_id` list filter that arrived beside the arm is honored and matches nothing, since no such actor is ever written (slice 2). The divergence is an absent arm rather than a wrong one — a client decoding with the SDK's union meets only variants it already handles — and it is registered here, at the bump that pinned the arm, rather than at the slice that writes the rows, because the pin is what makes the claim checkable. (`user_actor`'s id shape diverges for the separate reason the `principal`/`api_key` actor entry above gives: there is no `user_` id here either.) *Evidence: BetaManagedAgentsActorUnion, its doc naming all four members and the `AsAny` that dispatches on them (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaManagedAgentsActorUnion and BetaManagedAgentsActorUnion.AsAny), BetaManagedAgentsServiceAccountActor, its doc comment and the `svac_...` id (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaManagedAgentsServiceAccountActor), the `service_account_id` list filter (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaMemoryStoreMemoryVersionListParams.ServiceAccountID); the same union with three variants (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.63.1 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaManagedAgentsActorUnion); internal/identity (no service-account lane), internal/domain/id.go knownPrefixes (no `svac_`); docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 6 and "Out of scope"*
- **`/v1/dreams` is served (plan 41)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#475).** This entry replaces one recording the opposite — "No `/v1/dreams` — the memory-consolidation surface is not served", written when plan 36 scope decision 3 left dreams out — corrected rather than deleted so the convergence stays auditable. All five paths now answer: `POST|GET /v1/dreams`, `GET /v1/dreams/{id}`, `POST …/archive` and `POST …/cancel`, carrying the fourteen-key `dream` resource (the guide's illustrative create response shows thirteen — it omits `output_behavior` — while the spec's `required` list and the API reference's 200 example both carry it, and they govern). `drm_` joins `knownPrefixes` as `PrefixDream`, so a dream id is a well-formed id everywhere. The list takes the parameters the SDK and CLI send — `limit` (default 20, max 100), `page`, `include_archived` (default false), `statuses[]`, and the **exclusive** `created_at[gt]`/`[lt]` bounds this surface takes where the agent, deployment and memory-store lists take `[gte]`/`[lte]` alone — and pages newest-first behind a `{data, next_page}` envelope. The lifecycle rules are the guide's: cancel moves a `pending` or `running` dream to `canceled`, is an idempotent no-op on an already-`canceled` one and a 400 on a `completed` or `failed` one; archive sets `archived_at` on a terminal dream, is a no-op when it is already set, and is a 400 on `pending` or `running`. **A dream now runs.** The runner claims a `pending` dream, renders its input sessions' transcripts, clones the input store — or, under `update_existing`, mounts it — and drives a pipeline session whose id the dream reports, then settles `completed`, `failed` or `canceled` and archives that session — so `pending` is a state a dream passes through rather than the one it stays in, and the lifecycle above is the one a client observes. **Both output behaviors are served.** `create_new` clones; `update_existing` consolidates the caller's own store in place, held against a second live in-place dream by a partial unique index, and a create that collides answers the spec's own `conflict_error` — see the two entries below. *Evidence: the five paths (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamService.New and BetaDreamService.Get and BetaDreamService.List and BetaDreamService.Archive and BetaDreamService.Cancel), the fourteen `api:"required"` fields (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDream), the status union (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamStatus), the list filter and its bracketed array format (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamListParams.Statuses and BetaDreamListParams.URLQuery); platform.claude.com managed-agents dreams guide (the cancel and archive rules, the list defaults, and "a `running` dream can briefly report an empty `outputs[]`"); docs/plan/41_dreams.md §2.2, §2.3, §2.5, §2.6, §4.1, §5.1 and §5.3; internal/api/dreams.go and internal/api StartDreamRunner (the sweep behind the routes), internal/domain/id.go (PrefixDream in knownPrefixes), internal/store/migrations/0034_dreams.sql* *Landed for #475.*
- **`model` at create — no catalogue, no provider route, and no id-length bound on any surface (plan 41 slice 1, decision 8)** — The spec bounds a model id at 1-256 characters and the dreams guide names the six models its research preview supports; this platform enforces neither, and not only on dreams: `parseModel` is shared with `POST /v1/agents` and with a session's `agent_with_overrides`, so all three take any non-empty string (or an object with a non-empty `id`) of any length, unchecked against a catalogue and unresolved against `model_providers`. The rejected alternative is what makes this deliberate rather than an omission: `provider.Registry` is built in `cmd/brain/main.go` alone and the controlplane has never imported `internal/provider`, so a create-time route check means handing the controlplane `MODEL_PROVIDERS_PATH` and a registry for one `Describe` call — a new cross-process coupling for a check `POST /v1/agents` has never made either, and one worth filing for all three surfaces at once if it is ever wanted. An unroutable model therefore surfaces where the model is first called: for a dream, at the pipeline session's first turn once the runner slice lands, which the runner settles as `failed{internal_error}`. The 256-character bound is registered once here for all three surfaces rather than enforced on the newest of them alone, which would make one shared parser answer differently depending on which route reached it. *Evidence: "1-256 characters", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamModelConfig.ID, and `minLength` 1 and `maxLength` 256 on `id`, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaDreamModelConfigParams.properties.id; platform.claude.com managed-agents dreams guide (the six preview models); internal/api/wire.go parseModel, internal/api/dreams.go, cmd/brain/main.go (the only `provider.Registry`); docs/plan/41_dreams.md §5.2 and decision 8*
- **`model.speed` — accepted, echoed, and never checked against a model catalogue (plan 41 slice 1, decision 11)** — The SDK's own doc for the field says `fast` "provides significantly faster output token generation at premium pricing. Not all models support `fast`; invalid combinations are rejected at create time." This platform rejects no combination: `speed` must be `standard` or `fast`, and is then stored and echoed on the resource unchanged, exactly as it is on an agent — so a request the reference refuses at create succeeds here, and any mismatch surfaces at the provider instead. Which combinations are invalid is the reference's model catalogue and not this platform's: design principle 4 makes a provider any endpoint speaking the protocol, gateways and self-hosted models included, whose speed modes nobody here can enumerate — so a create-time check could only be a hardcoded table that is wrong for the deployments this platform exists for. *Evidence: the sentence this diverges from, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamModelConfig.Speed; internal/api/wire.go parseModel (shared with the agent and session surfaces), internal/api/dreams.go; docs/plan/41_dreams.md decision 11* *Tracked: #78 (a `speed: fast` create against one model that supports it and one that does not would settle which combinations the reference refuses and with what status. Plan 41's dream checklist lapsed with the plan, and the dream half is unobservable while the preview is closed: the agent surface carries the same field behind no preview gate, but nothing shows the two share a model catalogue, so a probe there settles the agent half alone. This platform refuses none of them on any surface).*
- **The session and dream lists accept a bare `statuses` beside `statuses[]` (plan 41 slice 1)** — Both the SDK and the `ant` CLI marshal the filter with `ArrayQueryFormatBrackets`, and the spec names the parameter `statuses[]` with `explode: true`, so every real client sends `statuses[]=pending&statuses[]=running`. The public list reference writes it bare — "Filter by lifecycle status. Repeat the parameter to match any of multiple statuses" — so this platform takes both spellings and unions them, on `GET /v1/sessions` and on `GET /v1/dreams` alike. It is a lenient parse of ours, the `session_` id alias's sibling: a client that followed the prose gets the page it asked for rather than a silently unfiltered one, and no client following the SDK can tell the difference. Whether the reference accepts the bare spelling is unobserved, and nothing here turns on the answer — accepting a spelling it rejects costs a caller nothing. *Evidence: the doc sentence, the `statuses` query tag and the bracket format, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamListParams.Statuses and BetaDreamListParams.URLQuery; the CLI's same format, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betadream.go handleBetaDreamsList; internal/api/sessions.go (the `statuses[]`/`statuses` union) and internal/api/dreams.go; docs/plan/41_dreams.md §2.5* *Tracked: #78 (`statuses[]` bracketed against bare, on the reference's own list. The sessions half is takeable now; the dream half waits on an endpoint this account cannot reach, plan 41's checklist having lapsed with the plan, and nothing shows the two lists parse the filter in one place).*
- **`memory_store_org_limit_exceeded` is never produced (plan 41 slice 2)** — The dreams guide lists six dream `error.type` values and calls the list non-exhaustive; one of them reports that an organization has run out of memory stores. No dream here can carry it, because this platform enforces no organization store cap — the clone a `create_new` dream writes is counted against nothing. The other five are all produced: `timeout` by the runtime budget measured from the dream's creation, `internal_error` by a terminated pipeline session, a stage over its turn cap, an unroutable model and the other faults the plan's table names, and the three `input_*` types by the checks the start arm and every later tick make against the input store and the input sessions. This is not an inference a recording could settle: whatever the reference answers when an organization is full, there is nothing here to be full of. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents dreams guide (the non-exhaustive error-type list); `BetaDreamError` types `type` as a bare string, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamError.Type, so the set is prose alone; docs/plan/41_dreams.md §5.2 and its out-of-scope list; internal/api StartDreamRunner*
- **`POST /v1/dreams` answers 500 `api_error` on a deployment that runs no dream runner (plan 41 slice 2)** — The reference has no such state: where dreams are served, they run. Here the runner is a controlplane sweep an operator can switch off — `DREAM_TICK_INTERVAL=0`, the chart's `controlplane.dreamTickInterval: "0"` — and a create accepted on such a deployment would sit `pending` forever with nothing to say why. So the create refuses, with `500`, `error.type: "api_error"` and the message `the dream runner is disabled on this deployment`, while list, retrieve, cancel and archive keep answering for the dreams a runner once ran. The status is 500 rather than a 4xx because the fault is the deployment's, not the request's, and `api_error` is the type the reference reserves for a fault on its own side. *Evidence: cmd/controlplane/main.go (the runner option installed only for a positive interval, and the log line that says so at boot); internal/api/dreams.go (the create's refusal); docs/plan/41_dreams.md §4.7; internal/api TestDreamRunnerDisabled*
- **A dream's pipeline session, and the transcript files it mounts, are read-only to the public API while the dream owns them (plan 41 slice 2)** — The guide exposes the session — "You can stream that session's events" — and says nothing about writing to it. Here every mutation a developer key could make on it is refused with a 400 `invalid_request_error` naming the dream that owns it: `POST …/events`, `POST /v1/sessions/{id}`, `DELETE`, the session and thread archives, and the three resource routes; `DELETE /v1/files/{id}` on one of that dream's transcript rows answers the same 400. Reads, the list and the stream answer as they do for any session, and the gate lifts the moment the dream closes, after which the session is an ordinary archived one. The reason is this platform's own and no recording can move it: the pipeline runs on a hidden agent whose toolset is `always_allow`, so a session anyone could steer would run whatever a caller liked under the platform's own agent — and, under `update_existing`, write it into the store the caller asked to consolidate. That a `create_new` session also has `bash`, and an `update_existing` one does not, changes the size of the hole and not whether it is one. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents dreams guide (the session is streamable, its mutability undocumented); docs/plan/41_dreams.md §4.3, §4.4 and §4.5; internal/api (the dream-ownership check each session-mutating handler makes, and the same check on the file delete)*
- **What runs inside a dream's session — the pipeline, its caps and its redaction — is this platform's design, not the reference's wire (plan 41 slice 2)** — The reference publishes a dream's inputs, its lifecycle and its outputs, and nothing whatever about what the session it exposes does. So everything between them is ours, argued in the plan rather than inferred from a source: the transcript renderer's filter (thinking blocks, images and the session's own lifecycle events dropped, a delegated child's report kept), its 24 KiB cap per transcript and 2 KiB per tool result, the best-effort shape redaction — `sk-`, `AKIA`, `Bearer` and assignment-shaped secrets — applied to every rendered byte before it leaves the controlplane, the `INDEX.md` beside the transcripts, the in-place run's narrower shape — no `bash`, so the write jail around memory is the file tools' own refusal rather than the prompt's, and a memory it retires becomes a tombstone because nothing left can delete — and the four staged instructions the runner posts as `user.message`s — orient, digest, merge, index and audit — each under its own turn cap (30 / 300 / 60 / 30 model turns, every thread's counted; the first, third and fourth measured against live runs, the second the fan-out's arithmetic) and each after the first opening by checking the previous stage's artefact, because the runner cannot read the sandbox and a container recreated after a crash starts with an empty scratch. A recording would show the reference's pipeline; it could not make this one wrong, which is what puts the whole area here rather than among the inferences. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents dreams guide and the API reference (the session exposed, its contents undocumented), with docs/plan/41_dreams.md §2.7 listing what is unpublished; docs/plan/41_dreams.md §3 and §3.4 (the two harnesses the design is informed by, read as design references only per CLAUDE.md); internal/transcript, internal/api StartDreamRunner*
- **A dream's digest threads are one per batch of eight transcripts, where the reference runs one per transcript (plan 41 slice 3)** — This is the single place a public artifact shows the reference's own pipeline shape, and this platform deliberately differs. The `anthropics/claude-quickstarts` knowledge-wiki notebook keeps two saved dream runs whose `session.thread_created` events number exactly one per input session — six for six inputs, four for four. Here the coordinator spawns one thread per **batch** of `dreamDigestBatch` (8) transcripts, so a hundred transcripts fan out to thirteen threads rather than a hundred. The reason is a cap of this platform's own: `maxLiveThreads` is 25 counting the primary, so one thread per transcript would refuse the twenty-fifth spawn on any dream past two dozen inputs, and the plan's bound is a hundred; batching also keeps the coordinator clear of the separate 25-turn settlement chain its spawn-and-wait round would otherwise walk. What a batch costs is context — eight capped transcripts is roughly 50k tokens in one thread — which is why the batch size is a package `var` rather than a constant. Nothing on the wire shows the difference: a dream's threads belong to the pipeline session, and the reference publishes no contract about them. *Evidence: anthropics/claude-quickstarts knowledge-wiki notebook at commit 40a7a14 (the saved runs' thread counts), the observation docs/plan/41_dreams.md §2.7 records; docs/plan/41_dreams.md §3.3 (the batch of eight, and the thirteen threads it implies at the hundred bound) and §9's wall-clock risk; internal/brain/delegate.go (maxLiveThreads, maxSettlementChain), internal/api (dreamDigestBatch and the stage-2 message); docs/HISTORY.md § "Dreams — the hundred-transcript bound" (the fan-out measured at the bound: thirteen threads for a hundred transcripts, not thirteen intended)*
- **`memory_store.created` / `.archived` / `.deleted` webhooks are never delivered (plan 36 slice 1)** — Creating, archiving or deleting a store fires nothing outbound, because this platform has no webhook subsystem at all — the absence [#261](https://github.com/OpenSDLC-Dev/managed-agent-platform/issues/261) was filed for when plan 21 shipped outcomes with polling and SSE as its only notification lanes. The store lifecycle is that gap's sibling, not a second one: whatever delivers `session.outcome_evaluation_ended` will deliver these three by the same machinery. Three is also the whole memory webhook surface — the reference states outright that individual memories and memory versions emit no webhook events — so slice 2's rows add nothing here. Until then the store lifecycle is observed the way every other resource's is, by `GET /v1/memory_stores` and `GET /v1/memory_stores/{id}`. *Evidence: the three `memory_store.*` event types, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betawebhook.go BetaWebhookEventDataUnion.Type; platform.claude.com managed-agents/webhooks ("Individual memories and memory versions emit no webhook events"); internal/api/memorystores.go (no emission path); docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md § Ground truth, "Webhooks"* *Tracked: #261 (the webhook subsystem itself; the outcome event in its title is the first emitter it needs, not the only one).*
- **Memory-version actors — `user_actor.user_id` is a `principal_` id, `api_actor.api_key_id` an `apikey_` id (plan 36 slice 1; the actor object renders from slice 2 on)** — The reference's `user_actor` attributes a write to "a human user through the Anthropic Console" and carries a `user_…` value; this platform has no `user_` id to give, because nothing here is bound to an end user (design principle 5) and its console is an IdP's, not an account store of its own. The human's stable identifier is the `principal_…` bookkeeping row the identity lane provisions, and a machine caller's is its `apikey_…` row id — the two lanes `principalFrom` already resolves. Slice 1 is where that choice is made, in the audit-only `created_by` column a store write records (never on the wire, the `sessions.created_by` rule); slice 2 is where the same value becomes visible, inside a memory version's `created_by`/`redacted_by` actor, the union arm following the lane that wrote it. So the arms, their `type` values and their field names are the reference's verbatim — only the id shapes inside them are ours, which is the shape difference the `service_account_actor` entry above already pointed at. *Evidence: the user actor's "a `user_...` value" and the API actor, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaManagedAgentsUserActor and BetaManagedAgentsAPIActor; internal/api/auth.go principalFrom (the two lanes and which wins); internal/domain/id.go (`principal_`/`apikey_` held out of knownPrefixes, being off-wire ids); internal/store/migrations/0028_memory_stores.sql created_by; internal/api/memorystores_test.go TestMemoryStoreRecordsItsCreator; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 6*

- **Memory-version retention keeps the newest five — the count the reference withholds (plan 36 slice 2, scope decision 5; implemented for #476)** — **OURS in its count, the reference's in its shape.** The reference retains a memory's versions for 30 days and adds that "the recent versions are always kept regardless of age, so memories that change infrequently might retain history beyond 30 days" — publishing the window and withholding how many "recent versions" survive. This platform sweeps at startup and then hourly for versions past the 30-day window that are not among their memory's newest **five**, and five is a number nobody can read off the wire: retention is a server-side background policy, so no recording against the reference can reveal it, which is why plan 36 deferred it rather than guessing at the time. One row is exempt whatever its age: a live memory's head, because `memories.memory_version_id` is not a foreign key and a swept head would be a 404 on a memory that is fine — the keep window already covers it, so the guard is belt-and-braces on the one statement that could ever dangle it. Nothing else is. A deleted memory's lineage prunes by the same rule, which leaves it listable rather than immortal: five rows of it always survive, so the lineage a client lists is never empty, and its `deleted` marker is normally among them — no version is ever *inserted* for a memory id after that one, since ids are random and never reused and a re-create at the same path mints a new memory. That is insert order rather than timestamp order (`created_at` defaults to `now()`, which is transaction start, and the delete's transaction begins before it takes the store lock), so ordering the marker out of the window is possible in principle and would take five such interleavings inside one delete's begin-to-lock gap; the listable promise holds either way. The reference's own "versions … persist after the memory is deleted" is about the delete not cascading, not about exemption from retention, and its retention rule names no exception. A redacted version is no exception either, so `redacted_at`/`redacted_by` — the only record that an erasure happened and who performed it — age out with the row they annotate. A store's hard delete still removes everything at once, through the `ON DELETE CASCADE` migration 0029 gives `memory_versions`. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/memory (the 30-day retention and "however, the recent versions are always kept"); internal/api/memoryretention.go (the sweep, its window and count, the head guard, and the index the per-candidate lookup rests on); internal/api/memoryretention_internal_test.go (the newest-five rule, the recent-and-few exemptions, a head the keep window does not reach, a deleted memory's lineage still ending in its `deleted` version, and several memories of one store settled by one statement); cmd/controlplane/main.go (where it runs); internal/store/migrations/0029_memories.sql (no retention column; ON DELETE CASCADE from the store)* *Landed for #476; the count is a standing choice, revisable only by evidence a recording cannot produce.*
- **A `self_hosted` session accepts `file` and `github_repository` resources, a superset of the reference's memory-store-only rule (plan 36 slice 6, scope decision 2)** — The reference documents that "self-hosted environments accept only `memory_store` resources"; this platform accepts `file` (plan 08), `github_repository` (plan 25) and `memory_store` (this plan) on `self_hosted` alike — a standing superset at the create surface, whatever the worker then does with each. The BYOC worker materializes `file` and, since slice 6, `memory_store` the same way its cloud executor does; a `github_repository` it still accepts and mounts nothing for, the deliberate no-mount of #322 (unchanged here). Slice 6 completes the memory arm: it landed the worker's memory sync and lifted slice 3's interim 400 (`memory_store resources are not supported on self_hosted environments yet`, judged from the environment's `kind`). A `self_hosted` session's store is mounted from the per-item sessions token its work items carry (`secret`, decision 15), and the worker fails the item with the reference's own `ErrSessionMemoryNoToken` when a store is attached and no token arrives — so the two workers agree on what a memory-less session looks like, and the platform never accepts a store it cannot serve. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/self-hosted-sandboxes ("self-hosted environments accept only `memory_store` resources"); the item failed with `ErrSessionMemoryNoToken`, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem, and the store download and the run-loop sync, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.Download and SessionMemoryStores.SyncIfDue; internal/worker/memory.go (memoryRefs, materialize, sync, ErrSessionMemoryNoToken); internal/api/sessions.go createSession (no env-kind gate on resources); internal/api/sessionmemory_test.go TestSessionMemoryStoreAttachment; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 16 and scope decision 2* *Tracked: #52 (delivered; the superset is a standing choice, settled when plan 36 archived — the repository arm's own no-mount is #322).*
- **Memory stores sync at the run boundary, not through a live mount (plan 36 slice 4, decision 11)** — The reference describes a cloud session's store as a mount whose writes other sessions see "almost immediately"; here a `cloud` sandbox's store directory is reconciled with the store when a tool run ends, before a run's tools read a mount the sandbox already held, and by the reaper before it acts on a sandbox — so a change made by one session reaches another at that other's next run, and a change another session or the API made reaches a running session at its next run — one run of freshness, not seconds. Two consequences follow and are this platform's: a `bash` write into a `read_only` store's directory is not refused by the filesystem — only the file tools refuse it — and is never pushed, so it is overwritten by the store's next change; and a session's write is attributed to `session_actor` only once its run has ended, never mid-run. The run-end sync is the reference's own self-hosted model at a coarser cadence: its worker syncs from inside its tool-call loop, at most once every 15 seconds, and once more at teardown. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/memory (the cloud mount's freshness); the 15-second cadence, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.SyncIfDue and SessionMemoryStores.syncStore and DefaultMemorySyncInterval, and the call inside the tool loop, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem; internal/executor/memory.go readMemory / settleMemory / applyMemory and reaper.go; internal/toolset/toolset.go Runner.MemoryRoots; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decisions 11-12 and scope decision 7* *Tracked: #52 (delivered; settled with plan 36 — the live mount stays out of scope).*
- **No delete corroboration window, no per-sync delete cap, no deletions mode — the wipe guard alone (plan 36 slice 4, decision 11)** — The reference's self-hosted worker guards a local deletion three ways before deleting the memory it mirrors: a 30-second corroboration window, a per-sync cap on deletions, and a `deletions` mode a store can be mounted with; plus a wipe guard that re-downloads an emptied directory rather than deleting everything. This platform keeps only the wipe guard: a file the agent deleted, against a store that has not changed it since, is deleted from the store at the run's end with a compare-and-set on the head's digest, and appends a `deleted` version — a deliberate, attributed, reversible-by-history act rather than a delayed one. The three other guards exist in the reference for a filesystem an operator's own tools may be sweeping between syncs; a platform-managed sandbox's directory is written by the agent alone. *Evidence: corroboratedDelete, the deletion cap and the mode, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.corroboratedDelete and deletePass.takeSlot and MemoryDeleteMode; internal/memsync/plan.go Plan (the wipe guard and the DeleteRemote arm); internal/executor/memory_test.go TestMemorySyncPushesLocalChanges / TestMemorySyncWipeGuard; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 11* *Tracked: #52 (delivered).*
- **A directory with files and a missing or altered marker is pulled into, where the reference leaves it alone (plan 36 slice 4, decision 12)** — Every mounted store directory carries the reference worker's marker, `.anthropic-memory-store` with the bytes `version 1\n<memory_store_id>`, and both halves stop trusting a directory whose marker is missing or altered. What each then does differs: the reference worker, finding files under a marker it cannot vouch for, leaves the directory as found and does not sync it at all; this platform keeps syncing it pull-only — the store's changes and deletions still land, nothing is pushed from it — so an agent that damaged the marker still reads a current view, and the file tools are not told (they refuse a `read_only` or archived store's files, and any path under `/mnt/memory` that is inside no store — never a marker's state). A wiped mount is the case both agree on: an empty directory against a baseline of more than one file is re-downloaded with a fresh marker (the reference's `stampAndPull`, our rebuild) and pushes again afterwards. *Evidence: scanMarker, markerSHA, the marker-mismatch return at the sync's start and stampAndPull, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.scanMarker and markerSHA and SessionMemoryStores.syncStore and SessionMemoryStores.stampAndPull; internal/memsync/tree.go MarkerBytes; internal/executor/memory.go materializeStore / settleStore / applyMemory; internal/executor/memory_test.go TestMemoryStoreMissingOrUntrusted / TestMemorySyncWipeGuard* *Tracked: #52 (delivered).*
- **The BYOC worker reads the store's archived and occupancy 400s specially, where the reference worker remembers every 400 as a per-file refusal (plan 36 slice 6, decision 16)** — The reference's self-hosted worker treats every `400`/`413` a push draws the one way: it records the file's SHA and leaves the file un-synced until its bytes change (`refusedSHAs[rel] = localSHA`, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.syncPath and SessionMemoryStores.upload). This worker keeps that rule for an unrecognized 400 or a 413 and reads two of the store's own 400s out of it. A `memory store … is archived` 400 turns the rest of that sync pull-only — the same end state the cloud executor reaches from the store row up front (decision 12), reached here lazily because the worker's sessions token cannot read the row and learns the archival only from the first write the store refuses; nothing is remembered, so the writes resume once the store is unarchived. A local deletion is the exception: an archived store admits a delete, as the reference's does (#685), so the worker's deletions — settled before its pushes — land on it, where the cloud executor's pull-only sync keeps them local. An occupancy-cap 400 (`memory store … holds 2000 memories`) is refused for that run but not remembered, so the create is retried on a later sync once deletions make room rather than pinned until its content changes.
