Both are safe-degrade: any 400 the worker does not recognize falls through to the reference's remember-until-the-bytes-change rule. *Evidence: `refusedSHAs[rel] = localSHA` for every 400/413, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.syncPath and SessionMemoryStores.upload; internal/api/memories.go lockMemoryStoreForWrite and createMemory (the `is archived` and `holds %d memories` 400 messages the worker matches on); internal/worker/memory.go refusalKind / settleStore (the archived and occupancy carve-outs and the pushRefused fallthrough); internal/worker/memory_test.go TestMemoryStoreRefusalsOverTheWire / TestMemoryDeleteReachesAnArchivedStore / TestMemoryPushRefusedIsRememberedUntilBytesChange; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 16* *Tracked: #52 (delivered).*
- **POST /v1/deployments and the update route reject `budget` (plan 37 slice 1)** — The reference stamps a deployment's spend ceiling onto each session it starts; this platform has no session budgets, so `budget` is absent from both allow-lists and a request carrying it is a 400 unknown field, exactly as POST /v1/sessions answers today. This one breaks a real client path rather than an obscure field: the `ant` CLI exposes --budget, --budget.max-list-cost and --budget.type on both beta:deployments create and beta:deployments update, so those invocations 400 against this platform. Storing and echoing an unenforced cap would be worse, because an operator would then believe a ceiling exists. The Deployment object never emits the key either, which is the reference's own shape for an unset budget — the one property outside its 16-entry required list, "absent when no budget is set". *Evidence: the spec's `BetaManagedAgentsCreateDeploymentParams.budget` and `BetaManagedAgentsUpdateDeploymentParams.budget`, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCreateDeploymentParams.properties.budget and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsUpdateDeploymentParams.properties.budget, and `BetaManagedAgentsDeployment` (17 properties, 16 required, `budget` the exception), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeployment; the create and update budget flags, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betadeployment.go betaDeploymentsCreate and betaDeploymentsUpdate; internal/api/deployments.go deploymentCreateKeys, internal/api/deployments_test.go TestDeploymentRejectsBudget.* *Tracked: #432 (the deployment half — a create carrying budget, and whether the Deployment object omits the key or renders it null when unset).*
- **Deployment and deployment-run webhooks — the nine `deployment.*` / `deployment_run.*` events are never delivered (plan 37 slice 1)** — The reference emits deployment.created / .updated / .paused / .unpaused / .archived / .deleted and deployment_run.started / .succeeded / .failed, each carrying only {type, id, organization_id, workspace_id}; this platform has no webhook subsystem at all, so none of the nine is delivered. Whatever delivers session.outcome_evaluation_ended will deliver these nine by the same machinery. *Evidence: the spec's nine deployment webhook data schemas — BetaWebhookDeploymentCreatedEventData, Updated, Paused, Unpaused, Archived and Deleted, plus BetaWebhookDeploymentRunStartedEventData, Succeeded and Failed — each a four-field payload of type, id, organization_id and workspace_id with all four required, and all nine carried in BetaWebhookEventData's discriminator mapping, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaWebhookEventData.discriminator.mapping; the same nine data types, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betawebhook.go BetaWebhookDeploymentCreatedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentUpdatedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentPausedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentUnpausedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentArchivedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentDeletedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentRunStartedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentRunSucceededEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentRunFailedEventData; a full-tree grep of this repo finds no webhook code.* *Tracked: #261 (the webhook subsystem itself; the deployment family is the second event group waiting on it, not the first).*
- **The 1,000-scheduled-deployments-per-organization cap is not enforced (plan 37 slice 1)** — The reference caps a customer at 1,000 scheduled deployments and directs the rest to support. A self-hosted operator owns their own capacity, and a limit we cannot tune for them is a limit that only ever fails a legitimate request. The cap appears in neither the spec nor the SDK. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/scheduled-deployments (fetched 2026-08-27 — "A maximum of 1,000 scheduled deployments is supported per organization."); the spec, which sets no maximum on any deployment count: none on the create params, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCreateDeploymentParams, and none on the list operation, whose one bound is a page size, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeployment.go BetaDeploymentService.List and BetaDeploymentListParams.Limit.*
- **A deployment configuring resources against a self_hosted environment is accepted and fires (plan 37 slice 1)** — The reference pauses such a deployment with self_hosted_resources_unsupported_error, because a self-hosted environment "cannot mount them". This platform already accepts a superset at session level — file and github_repository alongside memory_store on self_hosted — so the precondition the reference pauses on is not one this platform holds, and the type is produced by nothing. The consequence is real and worth stating: a github_repository on a self_hosted deployment materializes nothing and the agent is not told, so a schedule can produce a session whose repository is absent, every night. That is the standing no-mount of #322 under a timer, not a new gap. *Evidence: the spec's BetaManagedAgentsSelfHostedResourcesUnsupportedRunError and BetaManagedAgentsSelfHostedResourcesUnsupportedDeploymentPausedReasonError (both described as "The deployment configures resources, but its environment is self-hosted and cannot mount them"), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsSelfHostedResourcesUnsupportedRunError and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsSelfHostedResourcesUnsupportedDeploymentPausedReasonError, each carried in its union — BetaManagedAgentsRunError and BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsRunError.discriminator.mapping.self_hosted_resources_unsupported_error and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError.discriminator.mapping.self_hosted_resources_unsupported_error; docs/DIVERGENCES.md (the accepted superset, and the silent no-mount).* *Tracked: #322 (the deployment half — the same no-mount under a timer, so a schedule produces a session whose repository is absent every night rather than once).*
- **Archiving an agent is refused while a live deployment pins it, where the reference cascades (plan 37 slice 1, decision 7)** — A docs-page sentence says an archived agent archives its deployments in the same operation. This platform refuses instead: `POST /v1/agents/{agent_id}/archive` answers 400 naming the deployments whenever one with `archived_at IS NULL` pins the agent, and the operator archives those first. What we diverge from rests on that one sentence and nothing else — the archive operation carries `summary: "Archive Agent"` and no description at all, and a search of the whole spec for a cascade from an agent returns nothing — while where the reference *does* cascade it says so, the tunnels archive being the only operation whose description spells one out, at its current path and again at the deprecated Admin API path it supersedes. The spec's deployment types point the other way outright: `agent_archived_error` sits in both the run-error and the paused-reason union, described as "The deployment's agent was archived", which is a state a universal cascade would never leave anything in. So what is confirmed here is our behavior, not theirs. The trade is deliberate: a cascade destroys every schedule pinning an agent in one unrecoverable step, since this platform ships neither unarchive nor `DELETE /v1/deployments`, while a refusal is recoverable by retrying in the right order. Two carve-outs keep the refusal from building a dead end of its own. An *archived* deployment never blocks, being terminal and unable to fire — blocking on one would leave an agent no operator could ever archive. And an agent that is already archived skips the check and re-archives idempotently, because the refusal guards the transition rather than the state — refusing there would be a 400 no operator could clear by archiving the agent, since it already is. What the skip tolerates is a live deployment pinning an archived agent, and no route can produce that pairing: create and update both refuse an archived agent, so a *new* pin is impossible, and the archive of a pinned agent is what this entry refuses. It survives only as a row written before this rule, or by direct SQL. That is also what makes `agent_archived_error` unreachable for a deployment's own agent by construction rather than by convention, which slice 4's entry on that error type rests on. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/scheduled-deployments (fetched 2026-08-27 — "If a deployment's agent has been archived, the deployment is automatically archived in the same operation."); the spec's BetaArchiveAgent operation (summary only, no description), its BetaManagedAgentsAgentArchivedRunError and BetaManagedAgentsAgentArchivedDeploymentPausedReasonError (both "The deployment's agent was archived"), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsAgentArchivedRunError and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsAgentArchivedDeploymentPausedReasonError, and its tunnels archive operation (the only one whose description spells a cascade out, repeated at the deprecated `/v1/organizations/tunnels/{tunnel_id}/archive` it supersedes), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betatunnel.go BetaTunnelService.Archive, and the same bare "Archive Agent", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaAgentService.Archive; internal/api/agents.go archiveAgent and refuseDeploymentsPinningAgent (the FOR UPDATE the check runs under, against the FOR SHARE deployment create and update take on the agent they pin), internal/api/agents_test.go TestAgentArchiveRefusedWhileADeploymentPinsIt.* *Tracked: #78 (archive an agent that a deployment pins, and read both the agent and the deployment).*
- **Scheduled fires apply no jitter (plan 37 slice 4, decision 6)** — The reference offsets each fire by a small per-schedule jitter to spread a multi-tenant fleet's load. A self-hosted single-organization deployment has nothing to spread, so we fire at the first tick that sees an occurrence due, which lands 0-30 seconds after it and satisfies the reference's own published claim that a run starts "at or shortly after its listed time". scheduled_at still records the exact pre-jitter cron match, so the wire value is identical either way. *Evidence: "Each fire is offset by a small per-schedule jitter, so a run will actually start at or shortly after its listed time", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCronSchedule.properties.upcoming_runs_at, scheduled_at is the pre-jitter instant, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsScheduleTriggerContext.properties.scheduled_at; platform.claude.com managed-agents/scheduled-deployments (fetched 2026-08-27 — the 15% / 5-second / 9-minute quantification).*
- **session_rate_limited_error is never recorded (plan 37 slice 4)** — The reference throttles session creation and records the rejection as a failed run that does not pause the schedule. This platform imposes no session-creation rate limit at all, so the type's only cause never arises and nothing produces it; it is admitted by the store so a future emitter needs no migration. An earlier draft reused the type to mark a scheduled fire skipped for overlap, which decision 10 declined — this platform installs no overlap brake, so there is no second cause either. *Evidence: "Session creation was rejected due to rate limiting. The schedule keeps firing; subsequent runs may succeed.", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsSessionRateLimitedRunError, the type is one of the two the pausing union omits, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsRunError.discriminator.mapping.session_rate_limited_error and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError.discriminator.mapping; no rate limiter exists in internal/api.* *Tracked: #46 (per-org rate limiting on the management API; when it lands, this type gains its reference cause).*
- **mcp_egress_blocked_error is never recorded at fire time (plan 37 slice 4)** — The reference fails a run when an MCP server host the agent uses is blocked by the environment's network policy. This platform's address guard runs at dial time inside the executor, not at session creation, so a blocked host surfaces as a tool error inside a running session rather than as a failed deployment run. *Evidence: "An MCP server host used by the deployment's agent is blocked by the environment's network policy", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsMcpEgressBlockedRunError; internal/dialguard (its three live call sites are the vault probe, the MCP client's DefaultClient and the token-endpoint refresh — none of them session creation).*
- **workspace_archived_error and organization_disabled_error are never recorded (plan 37 slice 4)** — Both describe a scope this platform reserves but does not enforce: org_id / workspace_id / project_id carry single-tenant defaults on every top-level table, deployments included, and no query filters on them. The store admits both values so a future emitter needs no migration. *Evidence: the two run-error schemas, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsWorkspaceArchivedRunError and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsOrganizationDisabledRunError, both in the pausing fourteen, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError.discriminator.mapping.workspace_archived_error and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError.discriminator.mapping.organization_disabled_error; internal/store/store.go:34-42 (reserved, not enforced).* *Tracked: #56 (the emitter — plan 42 slice 6 pauses a deployment and fails a run with workspace_archived_error, and re-argues organization_disabled_error on one organization per deployment).*
- **skill_not_found_error is never recorded (plan 37 slice 4)** — Unlike the multi-tenant variants this one has a live surface here: skills are implemented, so an agent's skill reference can genuinely be missing. The fire validates exactly what POST /v1/sessions validates and no more, and session create does not resolve an agent's skill references — so a missing skill fails inside the running session's materialization rather than as a failed deployment run, and the type is produced by nothing. *Evidence: BetaManagedAgentsSkillNotFoundRunError, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsSkillNotFoundRunError, in the pausing fourteen, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError.discriminator.mapping.skill_not_found_error; internal/api/sessions.go createSession (no skill resolution); internal/executor/telemetry.go (skills materialization outcomes, which is where a missing skill surfaces).*
- **environment_not_found_error is never recorded (plan 37 slice 4)** — The reference fails a run when the deployment's environment row is gone. Here it cannot be: deployments.environment_id carries a foreign key, and DELETE /v1/environments refuses while any deployment references it — with a message naming them, which is the other half of this plan's change to that handler. An archived environment is a different type and is reachable. The store admits the value so a future emitter needs no migration. *Evidence: the spec's BetaManagedAgentsEnvironmentNotFoundDeploymentPausedReasonError, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsEnvironmentNotFoundDeploymentPausedReasonError; internal/api/environments.go:523-527 (the delete refusal this plan makes name deployments); migration 0031_deployments.sql (the FK).* *Tracked: #78 (delete an environment a deployment pins, on the reference, and read the error).*
- **unknown_error is never recorded, and an unclassified fire failure records nothing at all (plan 37 slice 4)** — Both published unions carry unknown_error as an explicit fallback variant — "An unknown or unexpected error caused the run to fail" on the run side, "An unrecognized error auto-paused the deployment" on the pause side — so the reference's answer to an unclassified fire failure is a recorded run that pauses the schedule. We roll the whole transaction back instead: the claim is released and a later tick retries the same occurrence — but only while it is still the most recent due one, which on a */5 schedule is the five minutes until the next occurrence falls due, not the hour the catch-up window suggests. After that the occurrence is counted as collapsed rather than retried, so a persistent fault abandons every occurrence it touches. The trade is deliberate. unknown_error is one of the fourteen pausing types, so emitting it would auto-pause a healthy deployment on a transient database blip, and this platform ships no auto-resume — an operator would have to unpause by hand. A rollback recovers unattended — the deployment, at least, if not always the occurrence. The cost is that the failure leaves no database trace whatever: no run row, last_run_at unmoved, no paused_reason, nothing in either list surface. The only record is telemetry: deployment.fires increments with outcome="abandoned" and the deployment.fire span is emitted either way. The store admits the value under the same CHECK as the other thirteen, so a future emitter needs no migration. *Evidence: both fallback variants, both documented as fallbacks, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeploymentrun.go BetaManagedAgentsUnknownRunError and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeployment.go BetaManagedAgentsUnknownDeploymentPausedReasonError; no auto-resume exists — of the eight routes on /v1/deployments only POST /unpause clears the pause columns, and there is no timed or automatic counterpart.* *Tracked: #78 (make a fire fail unclassifiably on the reference, then read the run and the deployment).*
- **A fired session is titled with the deployment's name (plan 37 slice 4)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#678).** The entry this replaces left a fired session's title unset, because no source said otherwise and because it was bundled with the metadata call now recorded as the `A fired session's metadata is empty` note. Two recording waves settled the title the other way: each fired a deployment, read the session back, and found its `title` equal to the deployment's `name` — `"rec78 nightly"` on 2026-09-02, `"rec84 two-adjacent"` on 2026-09-05 — beside a `metadata` of `{}`. The ownership-hook argument that keeps the metadata empty never reached the title, which is a nullable, human-readable label rather than a seam an application owns. Both fire paths, `POST /v1/deployments/{id}/run` and the scheduler, now pass the deployment's name as the session's title. What survives is one reading of ours: the name is read at the fire, so a renamed deployment's next session takes the new name, where both waves fired deployments that had never been renamed. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`deployment.run.session.get`, `deployment.create.cron`) and the 2026-09-05 recording (`rec84.session.get.before-delete`, `rec84.adjacency.two-adjacent`); session title is nullable on create, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCreateSessionParams.properties.title; internal/api/deploymentruns.go deploymentSessionIn; internal/api/deploymentruns_test.go TestDeploymentRunCreatesASessionAndRendersTheRun and internal/api/deploymentscheduler_test.go TestSchedulerFiresTheMostRecentDueOccurrence.* *Tracked: #78 (rename a deployment, fire it, and read the session's title); landed for #678.*
- **A success run outlives its session with its session_id intact (plan 37 slice 5)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#663).** The run object publishes "Exactly one of session_id or error is non-null", and the runs list publishes has_error=false as "runs with non-null session_id". Sessions are deletable on both sides, and the reference never nulls the link: a recorded run read byte-identically before and after its session was deleted, still naming a session that now answered 404, with `error` null — so the invariant holds for the run's whole life. The same wave answered the filter: `has_error=true`, `false` and a non-boolean value answered 200, 200 and 400. The entry this replaces recorded the opposite, because 0031 declared deployment_runs.session_id ON DELETE SET NULL and a deleted session left both members null; migration 0042 drops that foreign key, so the column is plain text that may name a session which no longer exists. The has_error=false arm still filters on succeeded_at IS NOT NULL — the durable marker migration 0032 added (#520), which the schedule.last_run_at entry rests on too — and on every run settled since 0042 that agrees with the published session_id predicate. **What survives is history**: a run whose session was deleted before 0042 lost the id, which is recorded nowhere, so it still renders session_id null beside a null error, and succeeded_at still counts it a success. *Evidence: the 2026-09-05 recording (`rec84.run.get.before-session-delete`, `rec84.session.delete.fired`, `rec84.run.get.after-session-delete`, `rec84.session.get.after-delete`, and `rec84.runs.list.has_error.true` / `.false` / `.bogus`); the has_error sentence, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeploymentrun.go BetaDeploymentRunListParams.HasError, and "no session lifecycle tracking", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentRun; internal/store/migrations/0042_deployment_runs_session_link.sql; internal/api/deploymentruns.go scanRun and listDeploymentRuns; internal/api/deploymentruns_test.go TestDeletingTheSessionDoesNotUnsettleTheRun; internal/store/store_test.go TestDeploymentRunKeepsItsSessionIDPastTheSession.* *Landed for #663; the success marker landed for #520.*

- **POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — `expected_last_heartbeat` is required here, optional on the reference (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78)** — A recording answered the question the inference this replaces asked, and the answer moves the entry rather than closing it: the reference accepts a heartbeat that omits the field, and omitting it is not "beat without checking" but a distinct, weaker operation — **claim if unclaimed, else extend** — which stamps `acknowledged_at` on the way. This platform still answers an absent field with 400, and that is now a deliberate divergence rather than an unconfirmed guess. The argument is the one the entry always made, and the recorded meaning strengthens it: requiring the precondition makes every heartbeat a compare-and-swap against the beat the caller last saw, so two workers cannot both believe they hold one item, where the reference's omitted form is a claim path that bypasses exactly that check. Requiring it rejects no real worker — the automated worker, the only real consumer, always sends it. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (the heartbeat probes, including the omitted-field form and the `acknowledged_at` it stamped); docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — the work-item lifecycle: get / ack / heartbeat / stop (slice 8, second part)"; omitzero, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaEnvironmentWorkHeartbeatParams.ExpectedLastHeartbeat.* *Tracked: #545 (whether to accept the reference's omitted form, which claims-or-extends with no precondition at all).*
- **POST …/work/{work_id}/ack — idempotent here, a 409 on the reference (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78)** — Only the queued-to-starting edge stamps `acknowledged_at`, so re-acking an already-advanced item returns it unchanged rather than erroring, which is what makes a worker's retry of a lost ack response safe. The recording settles the reference's half of that, in the opposite direction: both the ack of an already-active item and a repeated one answered `409 invalid_request_error`, "Work item sesn_… must be in QUEUED state to acknowledge. Current state: active." So the mismatch is real, and the retry-safety property is ours alone — a worker written against the reference must already tell "my ack never landed" apart from "another worker holds it", which is precisely the distinction our 200 spares it. Kept on that reasoning, and recorded here rather than deleted because the entry it replaces asserted the reference's behavior was unknown. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`work.ack` and `work.ack.again`, both 409 with that message); docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — the work-item lifecycle: get / ack / heartbeat / stop (slice 8, second part)".* *Tracked: #541 (whether to adopt the reference's 409 and give up the retry-safety property).*
- **Optimistic-version conflicts (agent/session updates) — the status and type match, only the message is ours (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78)** — The inference this replaces picked 409 with `invalid_request_error` because the reference SDK defines no dedicated conflict type, and flagged the reference's real shape as unobserved. The recording confirms the envelope outright — a stale `version` answered `409 {"type":"invalid_request_error","message":"Concurrent modification detected. Please fetch the latest version and retry."}` — so status, type and envelope are not divergences at all, and the entry survives only for what does differ: ours names the two versions (`agent version mismatch: expected 1, currently 2`) where the reference's names neither. Kept deliberately, because a client branches on the type and never on the prose, and naming both versions is what lets an operator see at a glance whether the caller was one revision behind or twenty. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`agent.update.stale-version`, request `{"version":1,"description":"stale"}`); internal/api/agents.go (the errConflict message), internal/api/errors.go.* *Tracked: #540 (whether wire-faithfulness should win and the reference's wording be adopted verbatim).*
- **DELETE /v1/environments/{id} — the self-hosted queue refusal and force=true (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78; implemented for #546)** — The reference refuses to delete a `self_hosted` environment whose work queue still holds items — `409 invalid_request_error`, "Cannot delete self-hosted environment with work in the queue. Either archive the environment first to allow the queue to drain, or use force=true to delete immediately." — and deletes it on the same request with `?force=true`. This platform now answers that refusal in that sentence: an undrained `tool_exec` item on a `self_hosted` environment — the work API's own scope, an item drained once `stopped` — is the 409. **What diverges is what force reaches.** On the reference the forced delete returned 200 with the queue live, and a live item there is itself a session, so no session stood in the way; here every work item carries a session row and `sessions.environment_id` has a foreign key with no cascade, so a delete forced past the queue is refused by the sessions — 400, "still has sessions; delete them first" — as one is by a deployment. `force=true` therefore exchanges one refusal for another rather than deleting, and that is deliberate: a session is the event log, and no resource delete here takes one down as a side effect of another's. It also means the 409 guards no loss the sessions' key had not already prevented — the delete raised 23503 before any cascade could run, and what the pre-#546 handler got wrong was the words, since clearing the sessions it named is precisely what cascades the queue away. The row the key cannot cover is an item whose environment differs from its session's, which the schema permits and no enqueue path produces. The archive path the sentence recommends drains the queue as described — an archived environment refuses new sessions, existing ones run on and their items drain — but draining is not by itself enough to delete here: the sessions that held those items still hold the environment, so they have to go too, which is precisely what draining first makes safe. The sentence is kept verbatim rather than corrected for this platform, so a reader following it literally can still reach a dead end — an already-archived environment is told to archive, and force never deletes. Wire fidelity wins there because a client branches on the status and the type, never on the prose. Neither the SDK nor the `ant` CLI types a `force` parameter on the environment delete; a client sends it as a raw query option. Four readings the recording does not settle: whether the reference refuses a `cloud` environment's in-flight queue too (the one cloud delete recorded, `env.delete.with-deployment`, held no queued work — this platform refuses `self_hosted` only, as the sentence says); whether a session alone refuses an unforced delete there (the forced delete answers only the forced case); which lifecycle states it counts as in the queue (the recorded item was re-queued, so no other state is observed — this platform counts every state but `stopped`, the boundary `queue.Stop`'s own force arm draws, so a worker's in-flight item refuses too; that is deliberately wider than what `…/work/stats` reports as `depth`, which counts the unreserved backlog a worker could poll next and so reads 0 for the leased item whose loss costs most); and what it makes of a `force` value other than `true` (this platform reads the parameter with `parseBoolParam`, the shared helper behind every `include_archived`, so Go's own `strconv.ParseBool` spellings — `1`, `t`, `TRUE` — force as well, and a malformed value is a 400 where before #546 any `force` value was ignored). *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 teardown, quoted in #546 — `DELETE /v1/environments/env_012x9DcYikKh1WcxFVA8evah` → 409 with the sentence above, then `?force=true` → 200, outside the exported arrays as the issue's provenance comment records; the SDK's BetaEnvironmentDeleteParams, which it generates from the spec's delete operation — headers only, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironment.go BetaEnvironmentDeleteParams, and no force field, absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironment.go BetaEnvironmentDeleteParams.Force; the CLI's environments delete, with no force flag, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betaenvironment.go betaEnvironmentsDelete, and work stop's `force`, the only one the CLI sends to the API, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betaenvironmentwork.go betaEnvironmentsWorkStop, beside `ant apply`'s, which overrides drift locally, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — pkg/cmd/apply.go applyCommand; internal/api/environments.go deleteEnvironment, internal/api/page.go parseBoolParam, internal/queue/lifecycle.go (HasUndrainedWork, which owns the scope, over workAPIScope and Stop's state boundary), internal/queue/stats.go (depth); migration 0001_init.sql (work_items ON DELETE CASCADE from environments; sessions.environment_id without one).* *Tracked: #78 (whether the reference refuses a cloud environment's in-flight queue, whether a session alone refuses an unforced delete there, which lifecycle states it counts as in the queue, and what it makes of a force value other than true); landed for #546.*
- **A memory attachment's element — `access` echoed as `"read_write"`, `instructions` only when supplied (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78; aligned for #666)** — **Converged, except on elements stored before #666.** The spec says `access` "Defaults to read_write" and types both fields nullable, and the entry this replaces read that two ways: the default stored and echoed as the string, which the recording confirms, and an `instructions` omitted or null rendered as `null`, which it refutes — the reference omits the key. `session.create.store-access-null` sent `{"access": null, "instructions": null}` and came back with six keys, `access` among them as `"read_write"`; of the 44 memory elements across the recording's two files, 8 carry `instructions` and none carries a null one. The same elements show `mount_path` never null and a store without a description echoing `""`, both as here. This platform now omits the key too. What survives is the old shape on old rows: an element is written into `sessions.resources` once and re-served verbatim by the session `GET` and the resources list, so a session created before #666 keeps `"instructions": null` on every read. No migration rewrites it — the SDK types the field nullable, so a client written against it accepts either form, and the brain's decoder reads both as absent. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`session.create.store-access-null`, `session.create.five-stores`, `session.get.five-stores`, `session.resources.list`); the spec's BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResource and …ResourceParam (`access` "Defaults to read_write", nullable), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResource.properties.access and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResourceParam.properties.access; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionresource.go BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResource and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResourceParam and BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResourceParamAccess; internal/api/sessionresources.go parseMemoryResource / memoryResourceJSON; internal/brain/memory.go; internal/api/sessionmemory_test.go TestSessionMemoryStoreAttachment / TestSessionMemoryStoreAttachmentRejections; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 7*
- **The memory mount-path slug — an ASCII alphabet, hyphens trimmed, and the whole store id's slug for a name without alphanumerics (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78; aligned for #671)** — **Converged, except on sessions created before #671.** Documented: the directory name is "the store's display name sanitized to a filesystem-safe slug (lowercased; non-alphanumeric runs become a single hyphen)". The entry this replaces inferred the three things that sentence leaves open, and one recorded create settles all three: "alphanumeric" means ASCII ("Ünïcode" mounts at `/mnt/memory/n-code`), a leading or trailing hyphen is trimmed ("(Notes)" slugs to `notes`, which is why it collided with "notes" there and landed at `notes-2` — the collision entry under INFERRED), and a name with no alphanumerics at all takes the slug of the **whole store id**, prefix included: the reference mounted "!!!" at `/mnt/memory/memstore-01d7kbgb3pwxq5cqhyg2tk8e`. That third reading was wrong here — the bare token, its case kept — and matches now. What survives is the old path on old rows: a session's element is stored once, `mount_path` included, and re-served and mounted verbatim, so an all-symbol store attached before #671 keeps its old path in that session; a deployment stores no mount path and derives the new one at its next fire. The slug is computed from the name snapshotted at attach time, so a rename changes later sessions only, which the reference documents. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`session.create.five-stores` — "rec78 Notes renamed", "notes", "(Notes)", "Ünïcode" and "!!!" in one create); platform.claude.com managed-agents/memory (the slug rule); the spec's `mount_path` ("Derived from the store's name. - **GET /v1/files/{id}/content — the environment-key content lane, wider than the reference's (confirmed 2026-09-03)** — A BYOC worker reads a mounted file's bytes with the same environment key it polls work with, over `GET /v1/files/{id}/content` — the **single** file route on the dual-auth set. It is deliberately narrower than skills' read set: the metadata GET `/v1/files/{id}`, the collection list, and every mutation stay management-only. On this lane the `downloadable` gate is skipped (a user upload is `downloadable:false`, yet a session that mounts it must read it), but admission to the lane is not sufficient: the key is **environment-scoped, and further gated on the file being mounted**. The download handler authorizes an environment key for a file only if some session in that environment references it in `resources[]` — one jsonb containment lookup (`resources @> [{"file_id": …}]`) filtered on `environment_id` — and a file no session in the env mounts answers 404, indistinguishable from absent. The scope is the environment, not the single session a worker happens to be servicing (an environment key is not tied to one session; it polls all of its environment's work), so the readable set is the union of `resources[]` across the environment's sessions — still bounded to the environment. Unlike skills (workspace-global shared assets), file content can be sensitive, so a leaked environment key is never a workspace-wide file-exfiltration credential and cannot even probe file existence across environments. The reference worker has no file path at all, so the lane and its auth scope are entirely ours to define and record. *Evidence: docs/plan/08_files.md decision 10; internal/api/server.go (isFileReadPath, dispatchAuth), internal/api/files.go (downloadFile, fileMountedInEnvironment), internal/worker/files.go (SetupFiles)* **The absence is confirmed, so the lane and its scope are now wholly ours — this is a divergence, not an inference.** An environment key was answered **404 `not_found_error` "Not found"** on `GET /v1/files/{id}/content` in all three recorded arms: the file as uploaded, the session-scoped copy the resource actually holds, and the same file under a key for a *different* environment. The middle arm is the one that settles it — a session in that key's own environment had mounted the file, and the reference still refused, so it is not a scoping rule we failed to satisfy but a route the environment key does not reach at all. The console cookie on the same file got a **400** instead: ``File `file_…` is not downloadable. Only files generated by a tool (for example, the code execution tool) can be…`` — the credential allowed to know gets the reason, the one that is not gets a flat, uninformative status. That asymmetry is deliberate existence-hiding: a 404 in every case means an environment key cannot tell "wrong environment" from "not downloadable" from "no such file", and so cannot use status codes to enumerate file ids or probe downloadability. **This platform's lane is strictly more permissive** — it serves a mounted file's bytes to the environment key with the downloadable gate skipped (decision 10) — and the mount entry says why the reference can afford to refuse: its workers never need an HTTP download, because files arrive as a per-session FUSE mount. The scoping this platform does apply is the right shape for what it chose to serve: unmounted and cross-environment reads are 404, matching the reference's own refusal to distinguish them, and a store error reads as "not mounted" so a transient blip denies rather than leaks. The cookie's 400 records one more thing for free — **only tool-generated files are downloadable at all**, an uploaded file never is — which bears on the file-download-headers entry, whose test assumes a downloadable upload. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `envkey.files.content-original`, `envkey.files.content-session-copy`, `envkey.files.content-wrong-environment` and `cookie.files.content-original`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §A.* *Landed for #55.*
- **Expired-file metadata — "up to 30 days" implemented as a floor, where the published phrase is a ceiling (plan 49 slice 2, #655)** — The docs publish both halves of what `expires_at` does: the content stops being retrievable immediately, while "Its metadata (GET /v1/files/{file_id}) remains readable for up to 30 days, with expires_at in the past". That sentence settles the anchor — the window runs from the expiry, not from the upload — so a file uploaded with a 90-day lifetime is readable for 120 days in total here, and that arithmetic is the published sentence's rather than a reading of ours. What the sentence leaves open is the direction of the bound, and the direction is where this platform diverges: **"up to 30 days" is a ceiling, and the sweep implements a floor.** The row stays readable for *at least* 30 days past the expiry and is then removed on the hour, so its real life is the window plus up to one interval — a backlog no longer stretches that, the sweep draining until a batch comes back short, though a transcript an open dream owns is held past the window entirely, until its dream closes (#698) — where the phrase as published would equally admit a reference that drops the row a day after the expiry. No recording can turn this into agreement, which is why it belongs here rather than among the inferences: if the reference removes the row promptly or lazily, ours outlives it by guaranteeing the window that sentence caps; if it keeps the row forever behind an expired marker, ours removes what it keeps. A recording sizes the gap, it cannot close it — a file uploaded with a short lifetime and read back past the window answers how the reference behaves, at the cost of waiting out the window. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/files ("remains readable for up to 30 days"); internal/api/fileretention.go (the window, measured from `expires_at` against the database's own clock); internal/api/fileretention_test.go TestExpiredFilePurge (a file one minute either side of the window); docs/plan/49_file-expiration.md.* *Tracked: #78 (how promptly the reference removes expired metadata, which sizes this gap without closing it).*
- **Skill reads on the environment-key lane — one route wider than the reference (confirmed 2026-09-03)** — The SDK's SetupSkillsFromSession doc comment prescribes the environment Bearer key for the versions list/get and `/content` download it performs, so those routes joining the worker dual-auth lane is client-side confirmed; this platform additionally serves the bare `GET /v1/skills/{id}` to the key for a coherent read-lane rule, and scopes nothing per environment (skills are workspace-global). The reference's actual server-side auth scope for these routes — and whether a management key is also accepted there — is unrecorded. *Evidence: the doc comment's WithHeaderDel("X-Api-Key") + WithAuthToken(environmentKey), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — skills.go AgentToolContext.SetupSkillsFromSession; internal/api/server.go (isSkillReadPath, dualAuth)* **The scope is recorded, and it is finer than "these routes".** One environment key was answered differently route by route in one pass — the four-route tally is in the resource-lifecycle entry below, and on the skills routes the split is: `GET /v1/skills` and `GET /v1/skills/{id}` are **403 `permission_error`**, whose message enumerates the scopes the token would need — `OAuth token does not meet scope requirement any_of(org:skills, user:developer, user:managed_agents, user:skills, workspace:developer, workspace:skills)` — while the whole version subtree, `…/versions`, `…/versions/{vid}` and `…/versions/{vid}/content`, answers **200**. `GET /v1/agents` is **401 `authentication_error` "Authentication failed"**, and `GET /v1/files/{id}/content` is **404**. Under the console cookie both refused skill routes answer 200, so the split belongs to the credential, not the route. Two things follow that no source had stated. The environment key is an **OAuth token carrying named scopes**, not the opaque per-environment API key this platform models it as — a coarser thing, not a wrong one. And admission is **per route**, not per API: the skill *version* subtree admits the key while the skill *resource* refuses it, and the two refusals do not even share a status code. That is the same shape #550 tracks on the work API, now seen on a second surface, which makes it general rather than a work-API quirk. The SDK doc comment this entry rests on is therefore right about the version routes and silent about the rest — and the widening declared in this entry's first half is now a **measured mismatch rather than an untested guess**: `isSkillReadPath` admits the bare `GET /v1/skills/{id}` that the reference answers **403** to, so this platform's read lane is one route wider than the reference's. That stays deliberate — the coherent read-lane rule is worth more than route-by-route mimicry of a scope model we do not implement — but it is no longer unobserved, and a reviewer should read it as a divergence taken knowingly. Whether a management key is also accepted there stays unobserved. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `envkey.skills.*`, `envkey.agents.list-should-refuse` and `cookie.skills.*`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §A.* *Tracked: #78 (whether a management key is accepted on the skill-read routes); landed for #54.*
- **Environment `config.packages` — installed per sandbox at every first provision, never cached across sessions (plan 40 decision 2)** — The reference documents the install as cached: "Packages are installed by their respective package managers and cached across sessions that share the same environment." This platform caches nothing across sessions. The lists install into each session's sandbox at its first provision, so a second session on the same environment pays the same `apt-get` again — and so does a restored sandbox after a reap, since the sentinel recording what a sandbox has settled (`/tmp/.map-packages`) lives outside the checkpoint roots on purpose and a restore is a fresh container. Inside one sandbox the pass is idempotent: a manager whose list is unchanged and installed is skipped, so the repeat is per sandbox, not per tool call. The reference's mechanism is unobserved; the cache it describes could be a shared package store or a per-environment image keyed on the packages hash, and it is the latter — the shape this plan weighed and declined — that is the delivery cost: Kubernetes has no image-build primitive, Docker's would need a registry both the executors and the chart trust, and the image would join the fields `sandbox.Spec` compares when it adopts a running sandbox. The divergence is therefore in cost rather than in contents — the same packages arrive, they just arrive more often — and it is registered because an operator sizing a package list against install time is sizing it per session here. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/environments § "Packages" (fetched 2026-09-05 — "cached across sessions that share the same environment"); docs/plan/40_environment-packages.md decisions 1 and 2; internal/executor/packages.go (packagesSentinelPath and its comment, installPackages), internal/executor/packages_test.go TestASettledManagerIsNotReinstalled and TestTheSentinelIsNotPreservedAcrossACheckpoint.* *Tracked: #595 (the per-environment install cache the reference's sentence describes).*
- **Session threads — a child's resume of an idle session (ours, deliberately; the recorded shape differs)** — When a child thread's move to running moves an idle session — a confirmation or result answering a child parked on `requires_action` while every other thread is idle — this platform appends, after the answer, `session.status_running` and then that child's own cross-posted `session.thread_status_running`, and the primary stays idle: the fold over live threads ("Session status as a fold over threads", among the inferences below) runs the session because the child runs. The reference does neither. Eleven resumes are recorded across six recordings, answering a child's ask with five `user.tool_confirmation`s (one a deny), four `user.tool_result`s and two `user.custom_tool_result`s. In every one `session.status_running` is paired 1 µs later with the **primary's** (the coordinator's) `session.thread_status_running`, ahead of the answer; the primary idles again (`end_turn`, with no model request between and no change to usage), and the child's own `session.thread_status_running` lands 0.14 to 0.57 s after the session event, never with a `session.status_running` of its own. In six of the eleven the child's running comes before the primary's idle, in five after it. In ten a `session.usage` and a `session.status_idle` follow the primary's idle, so the child runs under a session its event list shows idle, and that idle's `stop_reason` is `requires_action` still naming the ask just answered — stale; in the eleventh, where a sibling was already running, no session idle follows. The threads lists read just after five of the resumes show the answered child `running` and the primary `idle`. **This platform keeps its shape on purpose** (decided on #793, 2026-09-25): a session's `status` is a truthful fold that answers whether any work is happening in the session, and the executor's liveness check (a `tool_exec` item runs only on a running session), the MCP work check and the archive/delete guards all read it that way. Idling the session under a working child, as the reference's list does, would let an executor drop a confirmed call unrun and let an archive or delete end a session mid-work. The reference's shape is also inconsistent with itself — an idle written after the child's running in six resumes, a stale stop reason, an idle that depends on whether a sibling runs — which reads as an artefact of how it forwards the answer rather than a contract a client could rely on. The pair this platform emits is ordered by its session event, as the primary's is. The same fold decides what a wake of the coordinator writes while a child works, and that residual is deliberate too: a `user.message` that wakes the coordinator while a child keeps the session running writes the coordinator's `session.thread_status_running` alone, with no `session.status_running`, because the session never left running — where the reference, whose session reads idle while a child works, would write the pair. A consequence carries into delegation the same way: a child's report later wakes the coordinator without a `session.status_running`, because the reporting child still holds the fold at running, where nine of the ten recorded report wakes carry one ("Coordinator delegation", below). *Evidence: internal/events/status.go (TransitionThread, foldSession); internal/executor/executor.go and mcpwork.go (a live-work item runs only on a running session); internal/api/sessions.go (requireNotRunning); TestTransitionThreadFoldsOverChildren (the child resume, asserted as this platform's behavior); TestAWakeUnderARunningChildWritesTheThreadEventAlone (the coordinator's wake under a running child).* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings, each resume read off a session's event list resolved by probe name — 2026-09-02/batch2.json `sessK2.events.after-archived-subagent-message` (one, the confirmation at 00:09:51.927739Z); 2026-09-12-console-followups/setup.json `before-cleanup-main-events` (two), its threads list in approvals-network.json at 14:18:49.579Z (`alpha-approve-response`); 2026-09-12-child-lifecycle/setup.json `persisted-session-events-after-interrupt` (two, the second with no session idle), the same list the console reads in ui-network.json at 22:37:36.851Z (`old-viewer-idle-alpha`) and 22:38:12.412Z (`primary-idle-after-global-interrupt`) — confirmation `sevt_01UFLuBbv8ffy3jRmHG77BQB` at 22:36:33.010389Z behind `session.status_running` `sevt_015Nc7gNkrySJgYmYzYsnKzV` and the coordinator's `sevt_016y5YwnebNYAaHuZ3rYFXAc`, the session's `session.status_idle` `sevt_01AynFm4Z2f3vfTEQa3n6saK`, then the child's running `sevt_01DQnE1ivkB96LkYvkrzHDcG` at 22:36:33.346349Z — and its threads list at 22:36:34.647Z (`both-child-approvals`); 2026-09-19-self-hosted-docker/setup.json `multiagent.events.after-child-results` (two, tool results), its threads list `multiagent.threads.during-worker-execution`; 2026-09-19-self-hosted-latest-cli/setup.json `multiagent.events.after-child-results` (two, tool results), its threads list in ui-network.json at 15:22:18.779Z (`coordinator-threads-during-tools`); 2026-09-19-custom-mixed-tools/setup.json `custom-children.completed.events` (two, custom tool results), its threads list `custom-children.alpha-returned-beta-pending.threads`.* *Landed for: #793 (item 2, registered as deliberate).*

## Inferences to confirm (INFERRED)

Assumptions about reference behavior, each awaiting the recording that would settle it. An
entry no recording could settle is not an inference — it is a divergence, and belongs above.

A recording that lands does not always empty an entry. Where it confirms the assumption and
this platform already matches, the entry leaves for the notes section above, titled "no
longer an inference" as `agent_toolset default permission policy` is. Where it confirms the
assumption and we differ **by choice**, it moves up to the divergences. Where it shows we
are simply wrong, the entry stays here until the alignment lands, with `Tracked:` naming the
issue that will land it rather than the recording that already arrived — the question is
answered, the code is not, and a bug is not a divergence to be argued.

- **Work API auth — whether the wrong-environment 403 is uniform across the work routes** — The recording that settled the refusal itself (2026-09-02; the note above) exercised **`/work/poll` alone**: a live key aimed at another environment, and the same key aimed at an unknown environment id, both answering 403 `permission_error` "Token not authorized for this environment". This platform answers the other seven work routes — list, stats, get, update, ack, heartbeat and stop — identically, because all eight run one shared `workScope` check, and the tests pin that uniformity. **That the reference is uniform too is an inference**, and the recording cannot settle it even for the routes it touched, because the reference does not admit an environment key to all of them: `work.get` and `work.update` refused **the environment key itself** with a different 403 — an OAuth scope failure, "OAuth token does not meet scope requirement any_of(…)" — which lands before any environment comparison could, while `poll`, `stats` and `ack` admitted the same key. For those two routes the wrong-environment question therefore does not arise upstream at all. **That admission split is a mismatch of its own and belongs to #550**: the reference reaches `work.get` and `work.stats` with console org-auth where this platform reaches all eight with the environment key and none with a management or console lane, and a reviewer must not clear it on this entry's authority. What stays inferred here is correspondingly narrower — whether the routes the reference *does* admit an environment key to answer a wrong-environment key the way poll does. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`work.poll.wrong-env.cloud` and `work.poll.wrong-env.unknown-id` are its only wrong-environment probes; `work.get.*` and `work.update.*` carry the scope-requirement message against the environment key, while `work.get.via-console-org-auth` answers 200); internal/api/workapi.go workScope and its eight call sites; internal/api/workapi_test.go, internal/api/worklist_test.go.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference answers a wrong-environment key the same way on the work routes beyond poll that it admits an environment key to at all).*

- **Which console role may issue an environment key — `admin` here, unobserved there (plan 31 slice 2)** — The reference console offers a "Generate environment key" action, but which of its seven roles gate it is undocumented and was not captured in the 2026-08-10 recording that fixed the endpoint's path and payloads (plan 30). For **identity-authenticated callers**, this platform gates the whole environment-key surface — issue, revoke, **and list** — at `admin`; a management `x-api-key` is unaffected, because it is the deployment's root credential and carries no role at all (the role model exists for humans, and gating a key lane that has always meant full authority would be a different, unasked-for change). The gate rests on the local judgment that which hosts hold live credentials, and what they are named, is itself an inventory worth withholding from a read-only role. The reference's published role descriptions do say its *Developer* role can manage API keys, so a future recording may well justify relaxing this to `developer`; until one exists, the tighter gate is the one that fails safe. Both halves are testable against a recording: the roles the action is offered to, and whether the listing is gated separately from issuance. *Evidence: internal/api/server.go (the three consoleTokensPath/consoleRevokePath registrations at identity.RoleAdmin); internal/api/identitylane_test.go (TestIdentityLaneEnvironmentKeyRoutesRequireAdmin); docs/plan/30_environment-keys-console-issuance.md (the 2026-08-10 observation), docs/plan/31_console-sso-rbac.md* *Tracked: #78 (the reference's own gate stays unobserved — which of its seven roles are offered the key action, and whether listing is gated separately from issuance); landed for #56's SSO/RBAC half (plan 31 slice 2).*

- **The sessions token — when `secret` is populated, what it admits, and its scope (plan 36 slice 5, decision 15)** — Five readings of the reference the worker's behavior implies, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker, but no recording confirms — the first the kind recording item 10 settles, the rest readings of the worker's code no recording against the platform reaches. (1) `secret` is populated only for a work item whose session attaches a memory store: the SDK's field comment says "may be populated when polling for work" and the worker falls back to the environment key "otherwise" — a hedge compatible with always-on-poll and conditional alike, naming no condition; this platform mints only where the token buys something (scope decision 6). (2) The environment key is refused on the memory routes with a **401**: the worker's comment says those endpoints "reject the environment key" and names no status; here the memory routes take a management key or the sessions token, and an environment key gets the management lane's 401. (3) The token's admission matrix is the set of calls the worker makes with it — its own item's heartbeat and stop, its own session's read and events list/stream/send, the skill reads, and of the memory routes only the five its sync calls (the memories' list and create; a memory's get, update and delete) on stores its session attaches — and nothing else; whether the reference's token reaches more (the store's own lifecycle, other sessions in the environment, the rest of the work API) is unrecorded, and this platform refuses all of it (a sibling session and an unattached store 404 in the environment-key lane's shape). (4) One token per work item, superseded by a re-hand-out, rather than one per session: the worker reads it as "the bearer for this item's work lifecycle". (5) `read_only` is not enforced against the token: the worker enforces it itself (`ReadOnlyRoots`), as this platform's executor does on `cloud`, and the token's holder is the environment's own worker either way. A write through the token is attributed as `session_actor` (decision 6's sync lane, which the reference's actor union admits). The minute a token lives past its item's stop request is *ours*: the worker's post-stop flush (`Cleanup`, 30 s on a context of its own) needs it, and the reference token's lifetime is not observable from this side. *Evidence: the calls the token authorizes, the fallback, "reject the environment key" and ReadOnlyRoots, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem, the five `MemoryStores.Memories` calls, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.listMemories and SessionMemoryStores.pullAll and SessionMemoryStores.upload and SessionMemoryStores.deleteRemote, and Cleanup, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.Cleanup; internal/api/worktokenauth.go (the lane and its matrix), internal/worktoken/worktoken.go (Authenticate's join conditions); internal/api/worktoken_test.go TestSessionsTokenAdmissionMatrix, TestSessionsTokenJoinConditions* *Tracked: #78 (recording item 10 — a `secret` from a real poll: its key set beyond `sessions_token`, and whether a storeless session's item carries one).*
- **Which role each management route requires — the whole matrix is a local judgment (plan 31 slice 3)** — The reference documents that it has console roles and that they gate its UI, but not which role gates which API route; no recording captures it, and the entry above already records the environment-key case separately. So the matrix this platform enforces is inferred throughout, and three judgments in it are the ones a recording could most plausibly overturn. **Vault-credential reads are `viewer`** on the argument that they render sealed metadata only — the get and list queries select no `secret_ciphertext`, and the secret fields are split out of the auth document at parse time — where a reference that treats the whole credential resource as privileged would put them at its equivalent of `admin`. **The `admin` tier bounds secret access, not secret destruction**: a `developer` may archive or delete a vault, which nulls or cascades away every credential inside it, and may delete an environment, which cascades its keys — the tier gates who may read, write, mint or validate a secret, on the reasoning that destroying a container you own is ordinary resource ownership and discloses nothing, but a reference that gated the parent mutations too would be defensible and this platform would then be the looser of the two. **Session-resource create and rotation stay `developer`** although both seal a `github_repository` `authorization_token`, because mounting a repository into your own session is the session lifecycle that tier exists for and gating it at `admin` would put the platform's primary workflow behind the credential tier. All three are testable against a recording that exercises the reference console as each role. *Evidence: internal/api/server.go (the annotated route table); internal/api/vaultcredentials.go (the read queries), internal/api/vaults.go (deleteVault's ON DELETE CASCADE, archiveVault's secret_ciphertext = NULL), internal/api/sessionresources.go (the sealed authorization_token); internal/api/rolelane_test.go, internal/api/rolematrix_test.go; docs/plan/31_console-sso-rbac.md* *Tracked: #78 (the reference's own matrix stays unobserved — whether it gates vault-credential reads, the deletes that cascade a secret away, and session-resource create and rotation more tightly); landed for #56's SSO/RBAC half (plan 31 slice 2).*
- **MCP credential matching — what the normalization sentence does not reach (plan 29 slice 5)** — The documented rule is implemented verbatim: "scheme and host lowercased, default ports and trailing slashes stripped … a different path, subdomain, or non-default port does [prevent a match]". Everything the sentence does not name — path case, query, fragment, userinfo — is compared as given. Three readings it leaves open are ours, all settled toward *not* matching, since a false match sends a token to a server the credential was not registered for while a false miss dials unauthenticated and surfaces as an error: exactly one trailing slash is stripped; an IPv6 zone identifier is not folded, and two hosts are one when their IDNA A-labels agree — which still keeps `İ` and `i` apart, since they punycode to `xn--i-9bb` and `i` and Go resolves the two to different DNS names, while joining the spellings UTS46 defines as one name (`Ä`/`ä`, U+212A and `k`, `ſ` and `s`) that an ASCII fold left as two; and a tie between two spellings inside one vault is broken by credential id. Two normalizations the sentence does not name are ours and go the other way, because net/http applies them to the request itself so the two spellings reach one origin: an empty port (`host:`) is dropped, and a default port is compared as a number, so `:0443` is `:443`. Also ours: a vault that matched but whose credential cannot be used does not fall through to a later vault — the documented rule is about which vault *matches*. And the "no match dials unauthenticated" wording has one exception this platform does not close: an `mcp_servers` URL written with userinfo authenticates with the Basic header net/http derives from it, matched credential or not; a matched credential replaces that header rather than joining it, so the configured, rotatable one wins. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/mcp-connector ("Provide authentication at session creation") and /vaults ("Runtime behavior"), fetched 2026-08-14; internal/vaultresolve/mcpurl.go, internal/vaultresolve/mcp.go and their tests.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle how the reference normalizes what its matching sentence leaves unnamed — path case, userinfo, a second trailing slash); landed for #45.*

- **Which failures are authentication failures (plan 29 slice 5)** — The two error types and their causes are documented; the **test** is not. This platform reads an authentication failure as **401** on the operation's most recent exchange, and nothing else — 403 is a decision about the request rather than about the credential, and 407 is a proxy's status, not the server's. A recording settled that matrix **at the dial**, and the 403 arm was wrong here until #572 narrowed it: five servers pointed at `httpbin.org/status/{code}` in one turn answered 401 → `mcp_authentication_failed_error` and 403, 407, 500 and 502 → `mcp_connection_failed_error`, so only 401 is an authentication failure and 403 is a connection failure, whose message *there* is `access forbidden`. **The wording is the reference's and not ours**: this platform stores the go-sdk's prose for the exchange that failed, cut to `scheme://host`, so a 403 reads as a failed `initialize` rather than as `access forbidden`. Only the type is matched, and deliberately — 407's message shows the reference's wording is not a thing to copy. What the reference does with a non-2xx on the **call** rather than the dial is unobserved, the probe having varied the status on servers that answered it to every request; this platform applies the dial's split there too — 401 an authentication failure, every other non-2xx a connection failure whether or not its body carries a JSON-RPC error (#641; the answering-an-MCP-call entry says why). 407's exclusion holds for a different reason than assumed — not "not authentication" but a generic connection error, whose message leaks an internal type name (`ToolboxUnavailableError`). Three further facts arrived with it: `retry_status` is `retrying` for all five, the 401 included, so an authentication failure is retried rather than terminal (which this platform already does); an upstream 500 is reported as `upstream server error (HTTP 502)`, identically to a real 502, so a client cannot recover the origin's status; and `session.error.error`'s key set is exactly `mcp_server_name, message, retry_status, type` — there is no `http_status` field. The 401's message also states that the dial finds its credential **by URL**, which cross-confirms the `mcp_server_url` normalization entry. Two rules make "most recent" precise, both ours: the streamable transport's teardown `DELETE` is excluded, since it is sent after the operation has already failed and its success would erase the refusal that caused it; and an exchange that produced no response at all clears the flag, since a transport failure after a refused probe is a connection that failed. Both matter because the go-sdk opens every connection with a `server/discover` it recovers from on any error. That covers the reference's first two causes together, since a server answers alike whether a token was sent or not. It is observed in this platform's own transport chain because the go-sdk carries the status only as prose (checked against go-sdk v1.7.0 — mcp/streamable.go streamableClientConn.checkResponse), which a version bump may reword. A fourth cause is ours with no wire counterpart: a credential that matched and could not be resolved is an authentication failure and the dial is not made — an anonymous dial the operator believes was authenticated would hide the misconfiguration. `retry_status` is `retrying` for both types, per the connection-failure entry. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/mcp-connector ("Handle connection and authentication failures", fetched 2026-08-14); internal/mcp/authstatus.go, internal/executor/mcpcred.go, internal/executor/mcpexec.go (mcpFailure) and their tests.* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probe `session.events.list.mcp-httpbin-statuses`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §D.* *Tracked: #78 (which exchange of an operation the reference reads, and what it reports for a non-2xx on the call, are still unobserved, since the probe varied the status at the dial and not the exchange); landed for #45, #572, #641.*

- **OAuth refresh — when it runs and what a failed one means (plan 29 slice 5b)** — That refresh is platform-performed, and its grammar (RFC 6749, the three `token_endpoint_auth` arms, RFC 8707 `resource`), are documented; when it runs and what happens when it fails are not. Ours: at the dial, and only there — a credential is refreshed when its `expires_at` is past or within a minute of it, never in reaction to the server's 401, so one expiry costs one exchange rather than a refused call and a retry. The minute absorbs both the skew that makes `expires_at` approximate and the flight time of the dial it is about to authorize. An expired credential that *cannot* refresh — no refresh block, no refresh token — still sends the token it has, since `expires_at` is the issuer's estimate and not its decision; that is the reference's `no_refresh_token` shape carried onto the dial path. A refusal from the token endpoint is `mcp_authentication_failed_error`, and so is every other way a 2xx fails to yield a grant — an error document, a body that never finished, one past this platform's 1 MiB cap — and an endpoint at an address the dial guard will not dial. None of those is a retry away from working, and a retryable verdict on them has nowhere to surface: the work item faults, is reclaimed, writes no catalog row, and repeats with the operator told nothing. What does retry is the issuer having a moment: unreachable, 5xx, or one of the three 4xx that are about timing rather than the grant (408, 425, 429). The shape is the `invalid`/`unknown` split the `mcp_oauth_validate` probe makes, with two deliberate departures: those three named 4xx, because a probe's verdict is advice an operator reads while this one settles a tool call for good, and the guard refusal, which the probe reports as a plain connection failure on purpose so it cannot become an internal-host oracle. Rotation is stored under a compare-and-set on the ciphertext the resolution read, best-effort: the dial uses the token it was issued whether or not the write lands, and a refusal that follows another resolution's rotation — the row's sealed bytes having changed under this dial — reads that rotation back instead of reporting a failure, which is what keeps an issuer's single-use refresh tokens from turning a concurrent dial into a permanent error. **One dead end is accepted**: a grant that names no lifetime — or one so long this platform will not believe it — stores a null expiry rather than keeping one already in the past, which would refresh on every later dial; that credential then gets no further proactive refresh, and recovering after the server refuses its token needs the operator, because nothing here refreshes in reaction to a 401. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/vaults ("Runtime behavior", fetched 2026-08-14); internal/oauthrefresh, internal/vaultresolve/mcprefresh.go and their tests.* **One arm is recorded, and the method this entry implied for the other turns out to be impossible.** A credential cannot be seeded with a past `expires_at` at all: `POST /v1/vaults/{v}/credentials` with an `mcp_oauth` credential dated 2020 is a **400 `auth.expires_at must be in the future.`**, so the refresh window has to be reached by dating the credential seconds ahead and waiting. **This platform accepts what that 400 refuses**: `parseMCPOAuthCreate` reads `expires_at` for its format alone, so a credential can be created here already expired, which then refreshes on its very first dial (#589). Reached that way, with the access token past `expires_at` and the credential's token endpoint answering 400, the MCP call **still succeeded**: the platform sent the stale access token, the server answered on its own terms, `is_error` was `false`, and **no `session.error` was emitted**. So a failing token endpoint is silent on the wire, and the credential is matched by URL and its token does reach the server. **Ours is loud instead**: the same shape answers `mcp_authentication_failed_error`, because `refreshIfDue` marks a token-endpoint refusal `ErrCredentialUnusable` and the executor answers the call rather than attempting it with the token in hand — the argument for which is above, and it is worth noting that the can-not-refresh path here already does what the reference did, sending the token it has. Whether the failed-refresh path should agree with it, and so with the reference, is #590. What the probe cannot separate — whether a refresh was attempted and failed quietly, or never attempted at all — needs a token endpoint whose request log can be read, which is the same missing piece that blocks the MCP client-transport entry. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `cred.create.oauth-te-*` and `sess.create.oauth-dial-te400`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §D.* *Tracked: #589 (refuse a past `expires_at` at create, as the reference does), #590 (decide whether a failed refresh falls through to the stale token as the reference did, or keeps answering `mcp_authentication_failed_error`), #78 (whether the reference refreshes at the dial or in reaction to a 401, and which token-endpoint refusals it retries — both need a readable token endpoint, not another recording of this shape); landed for #45.*

- **Resolved toolset-config echo — what `configs[]` holds, and when it resolves (plan 29 slice 1)** — That the echo *is* resolved is documented, not inferred: the SDK marks `configs`/`default_config` and every `enabled`/`permission_policy` under them required on both toolset kinds' response types (three it calls "Resolved"), and the MCP-connector guide gives the two kinds one shape. Two readings around it are **ours**. **What `configs[]` contains:** the entries the client supplied, each resolved — not one entry per tool (impossible for the MCP kind, so both kinds share one rule); whether the reference instead lists every built-in on an `agent_toolset_20260401` echo is **unobserved**. It binds every surface shipping one of these objects, including a `session.updated` event's `agent` (`internal/api/sessions.go`, `materializedAgentSnapshot`). **When it resolves:** at render, not at write — unobservable from the wire. Stored rows keep the client's own bytes, so no backfill was needed, updates merge against what the client actually sent, and the default policies stay flippable constants (`toolset.DefaultAgentToolsetPolicy` / `DefaultMCPToolsetPolicy` — see the agent_toolset-default entry) rather than values frozen into old rows. **One place resolution is persisted, deliberately:** the `session.updated` payload is built before the event is appended, so that `events` row holds the resolved agent forever — that is what an event is, the record of what the API answered at that moment, and without it the same update answers twice in two shapes. Two consequences are accepted, not denied: an event appended before this slice keeps its sparse agent, and flipping a default-policy constant later leaves old events showing the policy in force when they were appended while the session resource re-renders under the new one. The invariant is therefore "resolution never reaches the agent and session **resources**", not "resolution is never written down". Wire-visible: a supplied value is echoed **as stored** (omissions filled, nothing validated), except an unknown *key* nested inside `default_config`/`configs[]`, which the rebuild drops (one at the toolset object's own level is kept) — both reach only rows written before this slice, since validation now rejects an unknown key at either level; and duplicate `configs[]` entries naming one tool merge field-by-field, last winning, echoing once in first-appearance order. **Since the SDK bump (plan 36 slice 0) a built-in entry also echoes `type`, unconditionally — request or no request.** That bump replaced the single resolved-config struct, absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfig, with an eight-variant union that dispatches on `type`, since anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfigUnion, so an echo carrying only `name` leaves a typed client with no variant at all; rendering it is the response schema's own shape rather than a guess.
